ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U1	Good afternoon, honourable Members of the Riigikogu. Let's start our final spring season briefing. Please, let's do the presence check first! 40 members of the Riigikogu registered to be present, 61 missing. In today's Question Time, the following members of the Government will take part in the duties of Minister of Defence Urmas Reinsalu Prime Minister, Minister of Agriculture Helir-Valdor Seeder and Minister of Education and Science Jaak Aaviksoo.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U2	Ladies and gentlemen, since Riigikogu member Rainer Vakra has announced that he does not want to ask a question registered for Question Time today, I would like to begin with a question that has been proposed by my colleague, Mailis Reps. As Mr Reps is not in the Chamber either, the question of Mr Allik must be started with the question of the Minister for Education and Science, Jaak Aavikkool. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U3	Minister, I'd like to thank you very much. According to the upper secondary school reform you are promoting, municipalities located outside Tallinn and Tartu are divided into four groups regarding the financing of upper secondary school education. Firstly, municipalities to which the state has set up a so-called "pure state secondary school" and is paying for the entire upper secondary school education, thereby giving a gift of about a quarter of a million euros each year to the municipality's budget in terms of schooling. Secondly, municipalities that are in line for this gift within six years and may lose about two million euros compared to the neighbouring county centre in six years. Thirdly, municipalities wishing to maintain a full cycle school and having to pay for the house expenses, support services and, where appropriate, teacher's wage supplements themselves forever. Fourthly, municipalities where upper secondary school education is not provided and who have to pay a contribution for young people entering municipal secondary school, but not for those going to state secondary school. The Chief Executive of the Cities Association, Võigemast, called this situation unfair competition. Does the Ministry see problems here and look for solutions or not?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U4	Good questioner! The situation you described has arisen under the current Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act. As we know, Estonia currently has state secondary schools, municipal secondary schools and private secondary schools. According to the current law, if a student living in the territory of a local government goes to a state secondary school, the local government does not have to give him money to cover the running costs of the school. At the same time, the Riigikogu has considered it reasonable that, in the case of pupils moving between municipalities and going to private schools, the local government is also obliged to bear the running costs of the school in relation to the fact that one student is studying in the territory of another local government or in a private school. The amendments to the basic school and upper secondary school law prepared in this section, which you will discuss, will not change anything. We have negotiated with local governments when establishing a state gymnasium in Viljandi and even reached an agreement with the city of Viljandi that the city of Viljandi will cover the expenses of young people entering the state gymnasium. However, serious difficulties have arisen in practical life and we are currently negotiating constructively with the city of Viljandi in order to abolish such regulation at the request of the city in Viljandi state gymnasium. I believe that the current regulation of the law could continue to apply in the future.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U5	Please, Mr Allik, the first point to be clarified.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U6	Mr. Minister! I understand the Ministry doesn't see any problem in this procedure. But for that, I'll keep asking. Your party partner, Minister Kisler, is currently promoting administrative reform based on attraction centres. You can understand that according to this model, there are about 50 municipalities in Estonia. The upper secondary school reform you have initiated is based on a pure upper secondary school model with at least 252 students, so you want to lose secondary schools in most of the attraction centres promoted by Minister Kiisler. Don't you think you and your colleague are in the opposite direction? Or do you mean that you can also call a city where only basic education is provided?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U7	The planning of the school network has also taken place in reasonable and quite constructive cooperation with Regional Minister Siim Valmar Kiisler and, of course, we have also reviewed the design of the school network through the prism that concerns the model of the attraction centres. The view that every attraction centre should have a middle-level educational institution, or upper secondary school, and, in parallel, a vocational training institution of that level, is obviously not justified. In particular, in the light of this reform, which has been carried out in respect of vocational training institutions, the number of vocational schools has been consistently around 30 in recent times, we can see that the school providing secondary education does not have to be in all areas, in every settlement or probably in every centre of attraction, which we estimate to be 50 to 60 in terms of quality education and coverage of the entire territory of the country. Therefore, I do not think that the attraction centres should link one-to-one with the existence of state secondary school, or even upper secondary school at all, if necessary, especially with the force of law.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U8	Please, Mr Allik, the second point to be clarified.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U9	Thank you, Minister, for your very clear position. However, in the previous Question Time, Mr Kiisler said, in answer to my question, that the funding of upper secondary schools should be based on an agreement between the state and the county councils on how many secondary schools remain in the county. All these remaining schools should be financed on an equal and school-based basis, not on the basis of the number or ownership of pupils. Why do you not support this position, but build the opposite model?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U10	Good questioner! Pretty much the opposite is artificial and completely unnecessary. As regards the financing of state secondary schools, this is based on the cost of maintaining the school, and there is a full consensus on this model. The funding of state secondary schools has not caused problems in the past, except that you have indicated that local authorities should also support state schools. We are of the opinion that it is not appropriate to maintain public schools by local authorities. As far as the more precise content of the financing model is concerned, as you know, the Riigikogu has proposed to separate the part of funding from this law, i.e. Article 82 of the PGS and, in this connection, the corresponding government regulation, so that there will certainly be a return to funding issues at both the Riigikogu and the government level. Now, in order to put an end to unnecessary speculation, which I must, unfortunately, read from the feathers of different people and hear from the mouth of different people, including, and especially in a preposterous form, some opposition representatives, I would point out that, of course, it is necessary to look for a solution for providing high-quality upper secondary school education not only in county centres. In Mulgimaa, for example, we need constructive cooperation to try to find a modus operandi for sustainable upper secondary school education, also along the Peipsi border and in many other regions. There is no reason to work on this and to intimidate people that these upper secondary schools will disappear from 1 September 2013 and some in 2014.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U11	Now the questions registered on the spot. Please, my colleague Andrus Saare!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U12	Minister, thank you very much. Yesterday, an article was published in the media by a former minister and a member of the current Committee on Culture. It should be a very competent and informed MP in the field of education. Among other things, he commented: "Today's senior teacher, who is at the same time the head of the class, will receive a hundred euros less after Aaviksoo's raise. ' Here's my question: how is it really going to be with the teacher's salary?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U13	I read it too, and I was embarrassed, to put it mildly. I think scaring people in vain is a practice that, even if there are political differences, should not be used. The amendments to the Basic School and Upper Secondary Schools Act are aimed at addressing one important issue: improving the pay and working conditions of teachers. Indeed, it is now possible that the schoolmaster, through the head of school, will reduce the income of teachers in the new academic year. This practice must end. The teacher has the right to the same degree of certainty about his contract of employment and salary as any other person working under the contract of employment. Therefore, the central idea of the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act is to guarantee a guaranteed salary for all teachers so that it cannot be changed unilaterally when the academic year changes, not to mention the change in the calendar year. Resisting this change is incomprehensible to me; it certainly does not serve the interests of teachers. As far as the teacher's salary is concerned, it must be determined according to his contribution to the work and the performance of the work, and it must only be able to rise. If this is to be lifted quickly, we must agree jointly to allocate the corresponding amounts to the state budget. As far as the classrooms are concerned, the money will continue to be allocated for its reward. There is no limit to this, such as the current 5%, which may be increased at the discretion of the headmaster, with the work of the headmaster being remunerated as an additional task to an additional 35-hour working week, be it five hours or ten hours, whichever is considered necessary.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U14	Please, colleague Marika Tuus-Laul!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U15	Mr. Minister! It has become the impression that during these years, when you have been the Minister of Education, there has been constant efforts to cut sums and internal redeployment, as well as to empower local governments. We remember that it was not at all long ago that the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act was passed in this Chamber, and now it has finally become understood and acted upon on the ground, but cardinal changes have already been launched again and you are denying previous positions on, for example, three courses of study and many other issues. Why are your views on the education system so volatile? And the other part of the question is why you no longer reach an agreement with anyone within the coalition. We know that there are currently no agreements on the financing model.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U16	The issue of education is complex and multifaceted, and it is difficult to make it clear in two minutes if there is no basic source information on such fundamental issues. But some of the false claims that were made on this issue, I am still trying to disprove. The funding for education has not decreased, but increased. Growth is when more money is allocated, and a reduction is when less money is allocated. If we keep up with the state budget, we will find that funding for education has increased and will continue to grow at least as long as this coalition remains. As far as responsibilities are concerned, I cannot mention any changes to the law that would have increased the responsibilities of local governments or schoolchildren during the period I was Minister of Education. If you refer to such changes, I ask you to be more specific so that I can answer you. As far as cooperation in the field of education is concerned within the coalition and not just within the coalition, it is very good. It is also very good that the funding section of the basic school and upper secondary school law was separated and returned to it in the autumn. This is very good because I believe that we will then achieve a greater consensus than has been the case so far. I also call on the opposition ambassadors to participate constructively in this work, just as they have constructively participated in the preparation of the draft law amending the law on basic school and upper secondary school, which you are going to second reading today.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U17	Please, Mr. Kadri Simson!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U18	Minister Aaviksoo! Last year, before the adoption of the state budget, you came up with a promise to raise teachers' salaries by 11%. It was a smaller amount than the teachers who threatened the strike asked, but still a pretty significant amount. However, in the state budget, the amount paid by teachers increased by only 4.7%. You said the rest of the salary increase would come from internal reserves. Now it's almost half the financial year. Where did the rest of the raise money come from to school?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U19	Good questioner, I've explained that too many times. But the teacher will have to explain until the message arrives and the understanding is clear. In view of the difficulties encountered by many local authorities in transferring public funds to private schools, the funds allocated to private schools in the financial year 2013 were allocated and transferred directly from the funds allocated to local authorities. So the numbers you compare don't have to match and can't match. I repeat once again that an increase of 11.4% was paid to all school teachers in the local government. We very much hope that the local authorities will pay out all this transferred money properly to the teachers. We know that some local authorities have not done so, but I know that they have promised to do so and are making efforts to do so. If that is true, by the end of the year, teachers' salaries will rise to 11.4% higher, as promised. In order to ensure that my complaint does not sound unfair to the local authorities who have already paid not only the entire state's money but also allocated additional money for teachers' salaries, I will give an example from here in Harjumaa. Not very wealthy, but Kernu municipality, which is educational, is able to pay more than EUR 300 extra for teachers, in addition to the EUR 800 allocated by the state, while the city of Tallinn, supported by more than EUR 1000 per teacher, has not paid more than EUR 100 per teacher.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U20	Please, Mr Kaia Iva!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U21	Dear Minister, As a member of the Committee on Culture, I have been working on this draft and have been in this process for nearly seven months. I have noticed that there has been a beautiful great consensus between the walls of the Committee on Culture in the Riigikogu between the opposition and the coalition, and the discussions have been quite constructive. How do you comment on this statement?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U22	Thanks for asking. Indeed, as far as the distribution of money between schoolchildren is concerned, the Riigikogu has delegated quite a large margin of discretion to the government in the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act. A regulation to this effect has been adopted every year when prepared by the Ministry of Finance, and this will continue to be the case if § 82 of the current Act remains in force. However, I would like to assure you that it is my sincere wish and that of the government that the topic of funding be reopened in the autumn in the Riigikogu, including a discussion of the government's regulation on the basis of this same rule of law. There is certainly no basis for stories, as if the executive power or any of his individual branches were going to be behind the back of the Riigikogu, passing by the Riigikogu to issue regulations that would seriously undermine the funding of local governments as schoolkeepers. Once again: the government will probably be able to complete the draft regulation even before the big summer vacation, at least one that could be discussed both in Parliament and in the government. I would certainly like to come back to this, because everything also needs to be discussed with local authorities as partners. True, we all understand that the local elections on 20 October this year create an atmosphere that may complicate the debate, but this certainly cannot lead to decisions being taken secretly, behind the back of the Riigikogu. So this debate is obviously ahead of us.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U23	Please, Mr Lembit Kaljuvee!

The current law tends more towards elite schools and disabled children are forgotten or, if not forgotten, the attention and care that have been given to them so far will be lost. Why is that?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U25	Good questioner! Such a high style, with a bright example of an excellent weekly newspaper in Midweek, probably does not help us to reach a constructively very far. Of course, the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act, drawn up by the Minister of Education and Science in the IRL ranks, takes into account the programme interests of the IRL as well as the objectives of the Coalition. This certainly also corresponds to the understanding that Estonia's educational public thinks is right and supported by the Education Forum. Once again, the law deals with specific regulations and changes what it changes. It increases public support for children with special educational needs and for children with disabilities. Increases support rather than stopping it or reducing it compared to the current law. If we come to the Riigikogu with a financial provision, there is a proposal to increase this support. Ladies and gentlemen, let us stick to the facts and specific texts. Let's not cause any trouble, because substantive decisions need to be made in a very calm manner, considering the pros and cons that are involved in all decisions and which are running firmly not along the borders of the parties, but along the lines of how well we understand the real needs and problems of Estonian education and are able to provide solutions to them.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U28	Thank you, Honourable Chairwoman of the Riigikogu. I'm so sorry, but I didn't know that Mr Reinsalu wouldn't be asked. First of all, thank you for your very thorough answers! There are differences of opinion about listening to people's talk, but unfortunately we also have to read the provisions of the transferred bill. It is now clear that one of the proposals to amend the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act came from the Government of the Republic is to lose the function of school management. This is not found in the new draft, and after the draft has been adopted by the coalition's votes on Thursday, the basic school and upper secondary school law removes the classroom management. Consequently, my first question to you, Mr Minister, is that. Today, you were very eager to convince the radio listeners that the local authorities naturally wanted to continue to fund it. How's that for real?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U29	Good questioner! Classrooming has been in Estonian school, is now and will remain in the future. I have emphasised, I stress today and I will emphasise in the future that the class manager actually has more of this work than the current 10% premium covers. I think that this work should be appreciated and given opportunities for it. The new basic school and upper secondary school law creates this possibility. It seems to me that we have a different view of the issue of whether all the things that are born at school, whether in terms of teaching class guidance or square root, should be laid down in the law, or whether there should be something left to the schoolkeeper, school caretaker, teachers and the director, including in matters of school organisation. We have proposed a number of changes that broaden the ability of the head of school to take decisions, based primarily on the guidance of the school staff, but also on the guidance of the school manager. If we look at the PISA studies at international level, we will see that the countries where the school's decision-making powers are not curtailed but increased by legal restrictions will achieve more successful results, and that is the line of leadership that needs to be put in place. I would like to stress once again: the schoolmaster has the right to pay an extra fee to the teacher for class management, not only 10% of the salary, but also 20% or 30%, depending on the organisation of the school's work, but also the possibility to leave it in the main job, so that the overall burden is 35 hours and, for example, the number of contact hours, which are included in his instructions, is reduced by five or six hours.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U30	Please, my colleague, Mailis Reps, the first point to be clarified.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U31	Indeed, we read the law differently. I would like to point out here that, as a schoolkeeper, local authorities certainly still have all the possibilities to interpret a number of provisions more useful to the teacher. So far, it has been right to start from 18 to 24 hours, but in most municipalities and schools, those respected schoolkeepers who are very energetically protected here have just started from a maximum of 24 hours. How do you expect to convince the teachers that the 35-hour limit that is now in force will not be applied to the same extent as now to be sent to class for 24 hours? What makes you think you can't send teachers to class for 35 hours from September 1st?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U32	There are schoolteachers and schoolmasters who set the norm for 24 hours, and they have schoolkeepers and schoolmasters who set it for 18 hours. The state is and will continue to rely on the relevant average of 21 hours, so much money will be allocated to the school and, within this amount, the Director will be able to recruit sufficient teachers to do the necessary work with a 21-hour load. If the organisation of work in one school is such that fewer teachers are employed there and their hourly load is greater than 21 hours, whether it be 22 or 24 or 25 hours, but this raises the pay of teachers, I believe that this kind of work does not jeopardise either the good quality of education or other educational objectives. Or, on the contrary, if the burden is to be reduced by equally reducing the average salary paid at school, it could also be up to the schoolkeeper. To be afraid that the director will tell the teacher that in the coming academic year you need to stand in front of the class for 35 hours is, in my opinion, inappropriate. Just as no hospital executive makes a surgeon operate 40 hours a week, so no schoolmaster puts a teacher in front of the class all the time. There could be so much faith in Estonian teachers and Estonian directors in this hall so that such threats would not go too far.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U33	Please, my colleague, Mailis Reps, the second point of clarification.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U34	We will certainly continue this discussion in this Chamber during the procedure of the draft law on basic school and upper secondary school, but the opposition in general does not want to threaten, but we are based on the facts. And the fact is that in cooperation with the Ministry of Education and Research, the Estonian Educational Workers' Association carried out a very thorough study in financing the Ministry, which gave the teacher an average workload of over 50 hours. So it's not a threat, it's a real life. The teacher's workload is already very high and there is a possibility that it will continue to increase as the workload is no longer limited by law. It has certainly been interesting to follow your statements in the media about new funding bases and your big promises. Today you have also said that local authorities have all kinds of opportunities. The real picture is that in many municipalities, the number of children is shrinking and funding is also decreasing from the budget. What should municipalities do with all these obligations?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U35	The schoolkeeper is obliged to cover all the costs of the school. This law has been in force since the early 1990s and this principle has not been contested by opposition parties either. In this case, we must continue with the regulation that we have by allocating state subsidies for the activities provided for in the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act. It is true that the amendments to the funding clause of this law provide for some differences, but I would stress that, in the basic part, the changes that should start in this Chamber in the autumn are more in the direction of the state taking on additional commitments rather than putting them in the hands of local authorities. I also want to draw attention to one simple truth. If there are no children at all, then there is no need to support school houses anymore, and if there are half as little children as before, then there is a need to support more or less schools and more or less teachers are needed for this study. Of course, this is a superficial statement, and I understand that if one or two children are less in class, there is no need to shut down the whole school or let all the teachers go. But we must keep pace with time. Teachers and schools need to move to where there are children, but not the other way around, that we are channeling more and more money and other resources to where the number of children is falling. Therefore, the education organisation in Estonia needs to be modernised and the money should be directed to where there is a need for it, where there are students, where there are teachers and where quality education is expected.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U36	Let's start by asking questions registered on the spot. Please, colleague Tarmo Tamm!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U37	Minister, thank you very much. According to the new PGS, a state secondary school is established in each county. We will move forward in Tartu, we will move forward in Narva and I think that over the coming years we will step-by-step design a balanced upper secondary school network where both state and municipal secondary schools have a reasonable and mutually agreed role, just as the vocational training institutions managed by the state and local authorities are operating successfully together. Artificial confrontation between different schoolchildren, incitement to contention between them is not a move towards a solution. ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U39	Please, colleague Valeri Korb!

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U41	I'm sorry, but I didn't get the bottom of this! The Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act has not been amended during the current government. It is true that changes are currently taking place in this Chamber. As regards the steps to be taken, they do not create additional obligations for schools and therefore do not require additional funding. No new tasks will be assigned to schoolkeepers, the performance of which should be further financed. We will do this knowingly as long as the commitments are not fully covered by the money. We cannot be satisfied with teachers' salaries, either minimum or average wages. As long as the current responsibilities are not adequately covered by the money, it is neither practical nor reasonable to increase the responsibilities of schoolchildren. How can we tackle the problem of insufficient spending? I have told you in this Chamber and I have repeatedly written in various publications that the biggest problem in Estonian general education is that we spend too much money on tasks other than paying salaries to teachers. Teachers' salaries must be a priority in substance, and this is a priority in the Ministry of Education. We have directed the money to raise teachers' salaries and we will continue to direct them. Taking on additional tasks, no matter how good faith and will it is, is not helping us to eliminate this worst bottleneck now. I therefore expect support from the opposition for these changes, which would enable us to raise teachers' salaries more decisively. The amendment of the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act is one step in this direction.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U42	Please, Mr Lembit Kaljuvee!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U43	Good minister! My question concerns the financing of education in comparison with other countries in the European Union. Estonia is doing well in the same way because we spend much more on education than the average. That's great! However, if we look at the internal proportions of education costs, the proportion of teachers' salaries is only 36%, 50% and more in other countries. What is your understanding of the draft that you are presenting to us in the autumn: are the proportions more European there or what is your plan in this area?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U44	Good questioner, this ratio certainly needs to be increased and there are opportunities for this. True, these opportunities are only possible if the state cooperates with the schoolmaster. We can improve this relationship and this figure is already higher than the existing 36% in public schools. In the Ministry of Education and Research, we have looked at the structure of education costs and found that in many schools there are still quite a variety of specialists in addition to teachers. These include essential support professionals, who have received additional funding from the state to pay, including through the European Union's Structural Funds, but there are still staff members who have long since not been part of the school staff in developed countries. I would like to give some examples, without any criticism of specific occupations in Estonian school. When we visit schools in Finland, we do not see wardrobe aunts. My question is, how many librarians do you have? They say we don't have them. We have emergency flights. These are a few examples. This is also about auxiliary workers such as cleaners, and there are plenty of boiler hunters and other professionals in our schools who have a special contribution in the field of education. So once again: we do not need the efforts of the Ministry of Education and Research alone; progress must be made in cooperation with the Ministry of Education and Research and school managers in order to move towards the educational organisation of developed countries, particularly our northern neighbour, Finland.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U45	Please, Mr Ossinovski!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U46	Dear Minister of Education, From your answers to earlier questions, you can see that you sincerely believe in educational self-regulation. At the same time, the University of Tallinn will not announce whether a person has been accepted until 25 July. How do you assess such competition? Do you think this is in line with students' interests?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U47	In order to comment on a particular case, it would take a little more to know than these facts quite quickly read out. In this respect, the higher education reform, or the law amending the university law and related laws, which was adopted here in the Riigikogu under the name of free higher education reform, has not changed anything. No university information has been received from the Ministry of Education and Research, or from the university as rector of my earlier activities, so I do not see any systemic and serious problem in this regard that would force the country to intervene at the level of the law. I would like to take this opportunity to draw your attention to one of the most worrying tendencies in the laws on education, and not just in the laws on this area. If you, good members of the Riigikogu, read the Estonian Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act or also the University Act and the Finnish Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act, you will see the huge difference between their level of detail and the degree of regulation. In Finland, the law mainly provides for qualitative courses of action, executive power and the school level decides most of the substantive issues on the basis of good academic and administrative practice. We have overgrown bureaucratic regulations in law, sincerely believing that this is a better solution than trusting our people and educational institutions. I believe that we should change our practice.

In 2012, a trailer with a maximum mass of between 4063 tonnes and 3,5 tonnes was first registered. The Road Administration is forecasting the same volume of first registrations for this year, with a 3-4% increase in volume expected for next year. The state fee for registering a passenger car trailer is unreasonably high. The average price of a trailer sold in Estonia is 640 euros, so the state fee makes the trailer 24% more expensive. At the same time, the new car registration fee is on average 1% of the cost of acquiring it. In Finland, for example, the price of the average trailer is 750 euros and registration with the license plate costs the owner a maximum of 29 euros instead of our 121 euros. In Norway, registration of the trailer costs only EUR 5.5 and in Sweden EUR 6.3. Our draft will contribute both to improving road safety and to saving the environment by becoming a law. I call for this bill to be supported. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U87	Thank you, dear questioner. A very good point indeed. If we are to undertake a constitution and our basic school and upper secondary school law, which we can discuss here today quite thoroughly, these documents, especially the Constitution, have been written to us that general education, which is provided by primary school, must be free of tuition. The Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act goes two or three more steps forward and points out that, apart from being subject to tuition fees, it must be free of charge. The Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act even states that a parent should not take money for workbooks and textbooks. The textbooks must be available free of charge to children. This is certainly a point that has caused quite a lot of anxiety and interesting discussions. What's really necessary when you go to school, it depends a lot on school and family. One way or another, you have to spend the necessary time buying pencils, erasing gums, some art objects, change shoes, sports clothes, sports shoes, these are those without which a child cannot study according to the school curriculum. Various publications, as well as experts, have counted each year on the minimum amount to be spent so that the child can be sent to school in September. Looking at the end of August and September last year's reviews put together by schoolteachers and other school-related people, it can be seen that the minimum amount of money (it was also taken into account that some municipal schools have, for example, art items, guasches, brushes, watercolours, free of charge, other schools require that the parents themselves worry about them) is oriental at EUR 100. There are also schools that hold quite a number of so-called school fairs, where you can get used clothes. Rather, it is that larger students offer their clothes to younger students, whether free of charge or for a small fee. Such solutions are offered by schools, but about EUR 100 must be taken into account by the parent. In order to make it a little easier for schools and parents to face these costs, we have a proposal, as I pointed out yesterday, that the minimum amount of support be equal to three times the rate of child benefit. We were based on the pre-crisis period, but of course in the future we should start to build on real minimum costs and gradually increase this amount.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U92	Thank you very much. According to the bill, which many really expect, the school allowance will be paid to a child aged 7 years by 1 October of the current year until he or she reaches 16 years of age. ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U93	Thank you. Good questioner! The reason why we pointed it out on 1 October is in the statistics we collected, and also in the fact that when money is paid out depending on the children's schooling, it is important that the data transferred to the information system also reach the pension institution, the local government offices and there are no errors here. Certainly, these children are important to emphasise this, who do not go to school at the age of seven, but need a school extension. It's a pretty thorough juniper that a parent has to go through, but it's possible. There are also parents whose child goes to school at the age of six. So a little flexibility is essential here, but the data will still come into the information system from the moment the child has started school since 1 September. Depending on the calendar year, 2 or 3 September may also enter into force on that date. In turn, the information system can issue these data to other agencies, in this case for the payment of family allowances to the relevant authorities. What is important here? If the child is older than 16 years of age and continues his or her studies, but at the same time he or she is very likely to have changed schools in the meantime, such as from primary school to upper secondary school or vocational education, then he or she will leave one information system and we will have to give him or her such a push to enter another system. At these moments, such as when a child has just entered upper secondary school, a situation may arise where the parent himself or herself has to bear these costs, in which case he or she will receive this money retroactively during the current year. However, since in general this system is automatic during school years and the child continues his or her studies within the scope of comprehensive school, he or she has no choice because the child has a duty to attend school and the parent has to guarantee it in accordance with our laws, we hope that this awkward situation will only occur on a few occasions throughout the entire school course of the child. It is justified to make no mistake. How does this system work? As far as possible, of course, automatically, but since automatic systems may not work perfectly from time to time, we have included in the bill that if a child does not go to school at the age of seven, for example at the age of six, or if there are any school exchanges, parents are obliged to inform the relevant institution about these matters in order to receive support on time. This is an additional guarantee that a parent, as an interested party, will give a signal if something has changed on the way to school. In the light of the electronic possibilities we have, parents should be able to apply for this support through the electronic system, and generally parents do so largely. On the other hand, there is such an opportunity at local government level that a person who is not on the Internet on a daily basis can apply with the help of a social worker. The system is flexible enough for every parent to get that money.

It is likely that this was not only a very optimistic parent, but also a rather well-paid parent, and it is very likely that his child will be lucky to go to a school where the schoolkeeper and the very intelligent management of the school have been able to provide these materials. It may also be that there is a child in this school whose parent is, for example, an importer of some educational material and has sold it at a cheaper price or even made a donation to the school. For parents in this hall, it is certainly not a strange matter that one parent wants to give a gift to the whole school, for example, brings colourful paper to the whole school, a very large amount of guasque colours, or something else, so that the parents of other children no longer need to reserve these things. But as a national system, the basic school and upper secondary school law has a guarantee that no money can be taken for textbooks, workbooks and worksheets. Moreover, due to the very long discussion of the opposition and coalition of the Committee on Culture, the new draft has specifically included, as a separate provision, that if there is money left over from textbooks, workbooks and worksheets, it can be used to provide other learning tools. Since this money is calculated per child and the classes use textbooks and workbooks in very different amounts, a large school with a hundred or more students can accumulate a quite considerable amount of money that buys art and sports goods or something else. There are also local authorities who have been very generous in providing educational resources. This is due to parents' problems again. When a dozen years ago, very many schools brought back school formalities, which I personally very much support, they quickly realised that the ability of parents to provide children with a school form was, unfortunately, quite different. This made it much more flexible, for example a vest or a sweater or a blouse, skirt length, a color gamma, but no longer specifically defined at what price you have to buy a school form. Speaking of school expenses, you can certainly mention sports equipment. A major dispute is, for example, about ski equipment. I am sure you are aware of this too, and I hope that the Committee on Social Affairs has at least discussed this briefly. Ski lessons are provided for in the curriculum. We have very specific rules and expectations for physical education teachers and results. But, for example, ski equipment that could provide a whole class and give sports lessons to parents for free is in very few schools. Practically, this equipment has remained since the sports bases separated their sports equipment. This is one very clear problem that schools have highlighted. The second issue, which has been up for quite a while in budgetary disputes, is swimming lessons. In its time, the state allocated 2.3 million kroons for swimming lessons. In euros, this amount has changed a little, of course it has been added, but every year there is an argument as to whether the country will continue to have very little money ♪ it is in the Ministry of Culture's budget to get out of the swimming lessons. In fact, it disappears from the local government's income base so that it actually guarantees swimming lessons for very few students. These contradictions tend to mean that if the school does not have its own swimming pool, swimming lessons are not organised at all. The municipality pays for the swimming itself or it is the situation that at least some parents have to bear a considerable proportion of these costs themselves. So there's a whole lot of costs at school that a parent who's not familiar with the life of a school student may not have noticed. I gave you a brief overview of some of the problems here, but there's a lot more of them. If the application side of the curriculum is taken apart, there is an enormous amount of precepts made to the school manager and the school about what the educational resources must be, what the child's supply must be. If, because of cuts in the school's income base or other responsibilities, the school manager is not able to guarantee it, the school manager is not able to take this money anywhere, but at the same time the basic school and upper secondary school law states that a parent does not have to cover certain costs himself, then the question arises whether the quality of education is suffering or whether the state is trying to step by step to come to school and also to help parents, supplying their children with, for example, sports equipment.

There would certainly be many among the parents and children of 60000 children who would feel that this school year is starting positively, but we will not affect the poverty line with this one-off, once-a-year support.

Therefore, there are also expectations and expectations that the local authority will be able to carry out a much larger number of tasks. It is certainly not unknown to most members of the Riigikogu that this type of support is used very much in Finland and Sweden alone. There are a wide range of child benefits, one-off and multiple benefits, except that this money is recovered from the wealthier population with progressive income tax. This means that there is a universal system, but in that sense, he is need-based, that if your income exceeds a certain limit, your family will not actually gain access to it at the end of the day. The grants will be distributed, but the money will be collected in taxes afterwards. It's a taboo thing again. We can give examples here and say that there is such or different support in this or another country, but if we do not look at it in the context of the rest of the tax system, this is a completely pointless example. In the Netherlands, for example, a large number of such subsidies have been left at local government level. Although the State has prescribed a framework for the types of grants to be paid, in what amount, this is the place where local authorities are attracted and can vary greatly. Only the distributions of the income base and the tax system are, of course, significantly different from those of Estonia. It is therefore extremely difficult to give you an example.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U184	In fact, no matter what we talk about in the Commission, when the opposition draft is under discussion, the ambassadors of coalition parties always arrive at the fact that we now have a development plan for children and families for the years 2012 to 2020 and that solves all the problems. It's strange. I would also like to say a few words about the development plan for children and families. When we look at what is written in it, we will see that it is clearly stated that the development plan for children and families 2012 has been aimed at all children and families living on the territory of Estonia, regardless of gender, nationality, race, language, religion, world view, social origin, wealth, place of residence and family form. Everyone must be able to grow and live in a secure and human-friendly environment. This development plan also states that children's and family policies affect almost every Estonian and are either indirectly or directly related to all areas of life from birth to death. The development plan attaches great importance to the fact that the contribution to the well-being of children and families is reflected sooner or later in public health, education, crime, employment and economic development. Studies also show that the child's growth environment determines to a significant extent his or her future prospects. Adults who have had a troubled childhood are more likely to be violent and antisocial, have mental and physical health problems, have low intellectual abilities and low educational attainment and may not be able to cope economically. When we talk about spending, whether we should spend more on children, giving them priority, it is important not to think that this EUR 28 does not mean anything. If a child doesn't even have a coat when he goes to school, so he has to go to school with a plastic bag, for example, others will know how to make fun of him, and he'll remember it for the rest of his life. The development plan states that the last 20 years have shown that dealing with the consequences, to a large extent, is not a sustainable solution; rather, it encourages a build-up of problems and therefore requires increasing funding. There is also talk of the situation that has led to the current child and family policies, and it emphasises that it has focused in particular on mitigating the symptoms of different problems, while addressing the causes has been more neglected. That is why the development plan focuses on a preventive approach and early intervention at all levels, which requires agreements between all parties, on which the well-being of children and families is also achieved. A common vision and a long-term consistent policy are essential. As Mailis Reps said earlier: if we give support one day, take the other day, it will certainly not work well for family security. In the same year of the cut-off, when parents were deprived of the opportunity to receive the package allowance before school began, the amount paid to the parent on the maintenance sheet was also reduced from 100% to 80% of the salary, which was also a step backwards from the point of view of families with children. Imagine a situation where a parent who receives a minimum wage must be at home with a sick child, so he gets much less money than when he goes to work. And if he has to buy another medicine for his child, he will probably have to choose whether to leave the sick little child home alone or buy him some medicine.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U231	Mailis Reps probably already introduced what the Government of the Republic thought about the draft of the Central Party Group on the amendment of the National Family Benefits Act. The school allowance is an annual allowance for children starting a new school year. The grant is intended specifically to cover the costs of going to school. The costs of studying and going to school are also incurred by families during the school year, so the government has decided to support certain target groups every month. From 1 July of this year, child benefit will increase to EUR 76.72 per month from the third child of the family. Children in families living below the absolute poverty line will receive needs-based family support from 1 July of this year. There is a mistake here, by the way, in fact it will be paid if it is a family living below the limit of relative poverty. From 1 January 2015, the third and more children's allowance will increase to EUR 95,9 per month and the need-based family allowance will double. These measures will help to ensure the daily livelihood of families throughout the school year. One-off support does not affect child poverty, nor does it have an impact on the birth rate. The implementation of the draft would lead to an increase in state budget expenditure of around EUR 4 million per year and no additional cost coverage sources have been identified in the draft explanatory memorandum. As a result, we had a discussion about how much local governments pay in the first grade, whether they are paid only to first-class children or whether they are paid to older schoolchildren at the beginning of the school year, and whether they are paid only to those who need support because they live in poverty, or whether they all get it.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U314	Honourable Chairwoman of the Riigikogu! Honourable members of the Riigikogu! Since the draft law amending the State Family Benefits Act, i.e. the first reading of the 435 SE, started yesterday, let me briefly recall within eight minutes what the purpose and content of this very important draft we have initiated is. The purpose of the National Family Benefits Act, as the Commission recalled today, is to ensure that the cost of caring, raising and learning for families with children is partially reimbursed. The content of the draft law amending the Law on National Family Benefits is the partial reimbursement of learning expenses. All children have the right to equal treatment. Children who grow in families whose income is below the statistical average should also be entitled to equal treatment. The school allowance provided for by the State Family Benefits Act 2001 was entitled until 2008 to receive each child starting the academic year. The school allowance was stopped in 2008 when the Victim Assistance Act, the Pharmaceutical Act, the Unemployment Insurance Act and several other laws were amended in one package. It was a package of cuts during the crisis. This bill was presented to us by the then Minister for Social Affairs, Maret Maripuu, who explained that the abolition of school support was due to the state budget crisis. The cessation of the payment of the state school allowance either pushed support for families in need of assistance to local authorities only or they had to turn to the non-profit sector in order to cope. The price increase that has already been mentioned here today also affects all families, but many families with children are particularly affected by it. Food and, of course, everyday utilities have become expensive. In the study 'Estonian birth factors 2008', the factors highlighted before many social scientists were confirmed. One of the factors contributing to the birth rate is certainly the tangible support of the state. But it is also the knowledge that if you lose your job or have to raise children alone or if children have to be raised, for example, by grandparents, then with the support of the state, children still have a full childhood and a good education guarantee. As a result, it is certainly very important to pay school allowance at the beginning of the academic year, even though it initially applied to the 2001-2008 period. 28 euros and 77 cents, converted from the kroons. The purpose of the school allowance is to provide support to parents to cover direct school-related costs. The purpose of the draft is, as I pointed out yesterday, and I also recalled today, to restore public school support. The public school allowance is intended to cover part of the costs related to the beginning of the school year; in addition to the office supplies, sports equipment, school clothes, footwear, etc. must be obtained. According to Statistics Estonia, 17% of all children under the age of 18 and almost 9.5% of all children under the age of 18 lived in relative poverty in Estonia in 2011. In total, nearly 60,000 children live in poverty in Estonia, and restoring school support is all the more important, the sadder the picture these figures are. There are several points in the draft which regulate different aspects of the award of school support. Paragraph 1 (1) contains a separate aspect of one-off family allowances, point 2 explains how and under what circumstances the school allowance is included in the family allowances. All children who went to school during the same academic year who fulfil the conditions laid down in subsections 5 (1) or 5 (2) of this Act shall be entitled to school allowance. The same point states that the school allowance is paid at three times the amount of child benefit, i.e. now EUR 28 and 77 cents. Point 3 states that the amount of school allowance is calculated on the basis of the child benefit rate in force during the month preceding the beginning of the school year. The State Family Benefits Act provides that the child benefit rate is to be set by the state budget for each financial year. Point 4 of the draft law sets a deadline for the granting of family allowances. Paragraph 21 of the Family Benefits Act is supplemented by paragraph 8 where the draft stipulates that the school allowance is granted to a child aged 16 years and older if the young person who applied for the allowance is also studying within six months after the beginning of the academic year. We also specify that, according to the draft, the school allowance is paid to a child aged 7 years by 1 October of the current year until he or she reaches the age of 16. Due to the fact that the school allowance is intended to cover part of the costs of starting the school year, the allowance is paid in August together with other family allowances. If the child starts studying before the age of 7 or studies after the age of 16, the school allowance shall be paid after receiving the data from the Estonian Education Information System or upon submission of the educational institution's notification or student ticket. If a child does not start studying when he or she is 7 years old, his or her parent must notify the pension institution in writing. According to our draft law, the law on the restoration of school support will be in force on 1 January 2014, which will provide an opportunity to take this into account when drawing up a new state budget. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U315	Thank you very much. I would now like to ask my colleague, Mr Pikhof, to speak as a representative of the Group of the Social Democratic Party.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U323	Let's start reading 375 third reading of the draft law amending the Code of Misdemeanour Procedure initiated by the Government of the Republic. I will open negotiations and ask for a speech chair as a representative of the political group of the Marika Tuus-Laul Centre Party. Eight minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U324	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, As I said at second reading of this draft, we do not understand why legislative changes concerning misdemeanour proceedings are being made in a hurry at the moment, because there is a very substantial draft being drafted at the Ministry of Justice, which is directly concerned with misdemeanour proceedings. The Supreme Court has also said so. In addition, we are currently working on a comprehensive law enforcement law, which also includes misdemeanour proceedings, an area that we want to amend today's regulation. The Minister of the Interior, Ken-Marti Vaher, explained that the purpose of the draft is to make the misdemeanour procedure more high-quality and simpler, but in fact it is the opposite. We believe that the problem is, above all, the shortage of police officers, the chronic lack of police money. This money could be gained, for example, from traffic offenders, as well as from the speeding up of the fine procedures and more fines imposed by the Tax and Customs Board. We are not at all opposed to punishing the guilty, but the question has arisen as to which degree we can still simplify the punishment and where to draw the line so that the policeman does not abuse his power. The proposed legislative amendment will allow the already small number of police officers to be further reduced. That's something the coalition doesn't want to agree with. At the same time, it has to be said that it was in this Chamber that the Commission's rapporteur Kalle Laanet stated that the amendment would give the police organisation tens of thousands of hours of work available. It is clear that two officials are now needed to fix an offence, a traffic offence, or some other wrongdoing. For neutrality to be guaranteed, as well as police safety, two people are needed. When these changes come into effect, only one policeman will do the job. It is therefore possible to reduce the number of patrol police officers, and the same officer in court can be both a source of evidence and a witness and a sanctioner. This could significantly impede objective justice and impair the quality of out-of-court proceedings. The amendment gives too much discretion to one official, and we fear that a personal level can play a role here. In addition, if the same person is both a judge and a witness and has to go to court, then the basic work of patrol officers, which is to ensure order in public spaces, will be seriously affected. It takes a lot of time to go to court. I recall that at the beginning of this month, Märt Rask visited the Riigikogu Hall, who stated that the number of appeals to court in cases of misdemeanour has increased. In 2012, a total of 6917 cases of misdemeanour had to be discussed by county courts, which was 35% more than in 2011. We know the court proceedings are slowing down. If we start sending a few police officers to the courts in addition to their usual work, there will be even fewer on the streets. As a result, the workload of other police officers will naturally increase. The Supreme Court also disagrees with this amendment, considering that "the developer may be a collector of evidence and their evaluator, but not a source of evidence for his future activities'. The other party to the amendment concerns the use of a photo or video recording as an independent proof. According to the authors of the draft act, the purpose of the amendment is to replace the minutes of the procedural file with a photo or video recording in order to avoid the need to draw up an additional document unless explicitly required by the infringer. At the same time, the Supreme Court stresses that the exercise of state supervision should not become a continuous recording of the activities of individuals, i.e. quasi-trace activities. In addition, the rule of law states that if a person does not agree with the decision of an out-of-court proceeding, he or she has the opportunity to apply to the courts. However, the courts shall require a record of procedural measures stating how the record relates to the offence and what other objective events are not reflected in the record. The protocol is therefore essential to get a full overview of the event and draw conclusions that either complicate or simplify the procedure. The current law has not ensured uniform interpretations. Practising lawyers have said that the change will make the situation even more confusing, which in turn may further increase the number of court cases in cases of misdemeanour. So we cannot agree in any way with Mr Pomerants, Chairman of the Committee on Legal Affairs, who said that one of the aims of this draft is to reduce litigation. It's definitely not gonna be like this. In summary, the Centre Party Group disagrees with these amendments. They are intended to give officials a chance to be very arbitrary, accusing the person and also imposing a fine on him. On the other hand, we are not willing to increase the workload of police officers who are already underpaid. We will vote against this bill. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U325	Thank you very much. I would now like to ask my colleague, Mr Anvelt, to speak as a representative of the Group of the Social Democratic Party. Eight minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U326	Ladies and gentlemen, I honestly say that when I prepared this little speech for negotiations, my aim was to draw attention to some of the factors that have attracted our citizens' interest since the publication of this bill. First of all, what have we seen on the streets in the last few weeks and months? The sudden spring and fast summer have again led to an increase in the number of victims and accidents. There is certainly one reason, as the previous speaker said, that we have few police officers. Today's draft law is an opportunity to send a lot more policemen to respond to the street. How does it work? Very simply! At the moment, when a person is invited to speak in a police car and to draft a protocol, I myself have not experienced it, I say it honestly, but I know it through many acquaintances and also police officers, then a long process, often up to 45 minutes, follows. It's annoying for a citizen who actually understands what his violation was about, because he sees it on the radar video footage, for example, and, secondly, it takes a lot of law enforcement time. The consequence is that two police officers sit in a car for 45 minutes, talk to a citizen and file a protocol, while dozens, if not hundreds of cars, and certainly some of them, are speeding up and putting themselves and their fellow citizens at risk. The purpose of this law is to speed up this process so that it can be done in 15 minutes, and only if the citizen insists, to make a protocol. The citizen doesn't even want to ask for it. All of us are human beings and, as Latin says, error is humane. We want to get back to our daily activities as soon as possible after the mistake, and the policeman wants to continue patrolling. Even now, when the case goes to court, there is usually a police authority in court. A person is usually appointed to do so in a misdemeanor procedure. When the dispute is upstairs, a police officer is called to witness. I hope that the same legislative amendment and the requirements that will be placed on recordings will often exclude this need from the future. Why is a police officer called out? Often to fix the place, the person who violated the right, and the speed of the offence. The requirements arising from this bill make it a very clear obligation to show on the record where something happened, how it happened, and what the first human testimony was. So the need to summon a witness may be exceptional, but even if it does, I see nothing wrong with it when police officers are sometimes called to testify in court. I have one question left of the whole bill, but life will show you how it is. The extrajudicial judge, or police officer, who makes a protocol of wrongdoing on the ground, is now liable to the law if he knowingly makes the wrong decision in the misdemeanour procedure, quite harshly. The Penal Code provides for a maximum term of imprisonment of three years. Now, if he goes to trial as a witness and lies there, then he's actually still being punished for false sayings. How does it work together now? But these are the legal gourmets' questions, and time will tell. If an official has misbehaved, he must be liable on both points: as a citizen as a witness and as an official as a decision maker. The fact that the film and other information recordings can be used as evidence is not a new thing in the Estonian legal space. It was already used by us when the Estonian police received their first video camera and filmed their first scene observations and other activities. Only now, 20 years later, will we bring this concept into the practice of misdemeanour, or bring our misdemeanour procedure to a much more modern level. I do not think that the citizens who we all are and who are always wrong from time to time will have any questions at all if the problems of the offence can be resolved very quickly on the ground. In fact, I also want to pass on to the Committee on Legal Affairs the thanks of some of my former colleagues, with whom I have discussed this draft. We also had a serious debate in the Committee on Legal Affairs. The draft law was received by the Commission at the beginning of the year. We listened to the various parties and made quite a lot of changes and improvements to the draft, and now it is relatively fine on our desk. I call on everyone, in particular, to reduce the number of victims and to allow the police to control the offenders more on the streets, to support this bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U330	The next item is the third reading of the draft law amending the Construction Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. I'm opening negotiations. I would like to ask for the floor as a representative of the Group of the Centre Party of the Olga Sõitnik.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U331	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The draft law amending the Construction Act was initiated by the Government of the Republic. a on 6 May. Amendment of the Construction Act...
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U332	Please, set up, Olga, the microphone a little closer to you! I can't hear you.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U333	The amendment to the Construction Act is due to the fact that Regulation No 305/2011 of the European Parliament and of the Council laying down harmonised conditions for the marketing of construction products and repealing Council Directive 89/106/EEC is fully applicable from 1 July 2013. Currently, the Construction Act contains a number of provisions which were necessary for the transposition into Estonian law of the Construction Products Directive 89/106/EEC, but which, now, would be contrary to this if the Direct Application of the Construction Products Regulation were to apply. I will address some of the points in the draft, which I believe are the most important and on which questions have also been put in this Chamber during the procedure. First, § 3 of the Act is supplemented by paragraphs 74 and 75. From 1 June, Regulation No 305/2011, which applies directly, lays down requirements for construction products, shall apply and, in that context, the provisions relating to construction products in paragraphs 5 to 8 shall be deleted. For reasons of legal clarity, paragraph 74 introduces a reference to the Regulation and the Product Conformity Act. In this case, the addressee of the standard can also understand where the requirements for construction products come from. Paragraph 75 provides that permanent installation of non-industrially untreated natural construction material, a traditional national construction product or a recycled construction product in a building must ensure that the building complies with the requirements of § 3 (2) (7). In essence, this is an addition to the current § 6 (2) by providing for the use of natural and traditional building materials in buildings. Industrially untreated natural building material or traditionally manufactured national construction material or product are for example mass log products, strawballs, clay, cane, natural stones, forgings and other alternative materials and products not manufactured industrially as large lots. It is necessary to include this provision in the Construction Act, since the construction materials and articles mentioned above do not fall within the scope of the Construction Products Regulation, i.e. that no harmonised technical specifications have been drawn up for them and that there is no obligation to use the CE marking for such products and materials. It also supports the principles of the Construction Products Regulation on sustainable and environmentally friendly construction. In addition, § 9 is introduced in a new wording. This section governs the granting of the right to act as TAB. The technical assessment body shall be the conformity assessment body which, at the request of the manufacturer, performs a European technical assessment of the product on the basis of the European Assessment Document, on the basis of which the manufacturer can draw up a declaration of performance for the product and affix the CE marking. In the current wording, paragraphs 1 and 2 are deleted because, with effect from 1 July 2013, Council Directive 89/106/EEC is repealed and the Construction Products Regulation is fully applicable. Existing subsections 9 (3) and 9 (4) become paragraphs 1 and 2. In paragraph 1, reference is made to the Construction Products Regulation and in both paragraphs the term'recognition authority' under the Construction Products Directive is replaced by 'technical assessment body'. There has been a change in the procedure for granting the right to act as TAB. According to the procedure previously in force, the right to act as an institution of recognition was granted by the Government of the Republic to one person on a proposal from the Minister of Economic Affairs and Communications on the basis of an administrative agreement. According to the new wording of the provision, the right to act as TAB is granted by the Minister for Economic Affairs and Communications. The draft states that, in accordance with § 9 (3), upon receipt of the application, the Minister shall set up a committee to assess whether the applicant fulfils the requirements set out in the table in Annex 2 to the Construction Products Regulation. The number of TAB actors is no longer limited. So far, there could have been only one and there was only one TUT. There are currently no technical assessment bodies or recognition bodies in Estonia. I'm asking for three more minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U334	Three extra minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U335	These were some of the most important points. The first reading of this draft law took place in this Chamber on 22 May and the draft was not explained by the Minister of Economic Affairs and Communications Juhan Parts, but by the Minister of Agriculture Helir-Valdor Seeder. At this meeting, it was agreed that the deadline for tabling amendments would be 29 May. No amendments were received from political groups or delegates at that time. On 30 May a working group of the Economic Commission met to prepare the draft for the Commission's meeting. The Committee on Economic Affairs discussed the draft for the preparation of the second reading at its meeting on 3 June, which was also attended by a representative of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. The Commission decided to make two amendments, one from the Committee on Economic Affairs and the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, and the other from the Group's proposals. Amendment 1 was such that § 1 of the draft could be supplemented by one point amending the norm comment of the Construction Act. Since, pursuant to Articles 65 and 68 of Regulation No 305/211 of the European Parliament and of the Council, it is recognised as from s. a 1 July repealing Council Directive 89/106/EEC, the reference to that Directive shall be deleted from the technical note of the Construction Act. Directive 2002/91/EC is already invalid and the technical notes need to be updated in this respect. Directive 2002/91/EC replaces Directive 2010/31/EU. This amendment was tabled by the Committee on Economic Affairs and the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. Amendment 2 was such that the Commission's proposal is to amend the date of entry into force of the law by deleting the entry into force provision. As the deadline for the entry into force of the Act in § 2 of the draft law s. a 1 July is not real in view of the late submission of the draft, it is excluded from the draft. Although Regulation No 305/2011 of the European Parliament and of the Council, which directly introduced the amendment to the Construction Act, enters into force in full s. a On 1 July, the entry into force of the law after that date shall not give rise to any conflict to an extent which could cause serious confusion to the addressee of the rule or lead to infringement proceedings. With the exception of § 2 of the Bill, the Act enters into force in general terms. The Committee on Economic Affairs supported both of these amendments and decided to submit the draft for second reading on the agenda of the plenary session of the Riigikogu by 12 June and to propose that the second reading be terminated. It was further decided that, once the second reading of the draft was completed, the third reading of the draft could be carried out on 19 June.
