ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U340	The third reading of the draft law amending the Law on Feedingstuffs initiated by the Government of the Republic begins. Do the representatives of the political groups wish to open negotiations? Please, Mr Kalev Eagle, as representative of the Social Democratic Party Group.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U341	Eight minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U342	Eight minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U343	Honor the President of the sitting! Ladies and gentlemen, Although the drafting of a draft law amending the Law on Feedingstuffs is due to the need to ensure the effective implementation of the obligations under European Union law, it is not purely technical. The final objective of the amendments to the feed law is to ensure a high level of traceability and consumer protection throughout the feed and food chain, to improve feed hygiene and to reduce the risk of contaminated products entering the food chain. Perhaps high-quality feed must provide high-quality food. Of course, it must be observed that the law of the Estonian state does not contain regulation contrary to European Union law, nor does it duplicate the provisions of the regulations of the European Parliament and of the Council. Today, however, the European Parliament and the Council have, in a sense, forced us to do so. Those institutions have confirmed the second programme of action on transmissible spongiform encephalopathies (TSEs), the objective of which is to continue reviewing TSE-related measures for the years 2010 to 2015, while ensuring a high level of food safety. However, amendments to the TSE-related rules are now and will continue to be made gradually, on the basis of reliable scientific data. This means that national parliaments will also be able to harmonise their legislation in this area now and also in the future gradually at a dictated pace of the European Parliament and the Council. It is good that the first strategic objectives of the TSE action programme are a review of some of the measures of the current complete ban on feed, provided that certain conditions are met. For example, processed animal protein currently banned in feed is mainly used for the production of fuel used in fertiliser, compost and cement plants, while the animal body also needs protein for normal function and production as a single nutrient. The European Food Safety Authority has already issued an opinion in 2007 showing that no TSE has been detected in non-ruminant farmed animals under natural conditions and that the risk of transmission of bovine spongiform encephalopathy from non-ruminants to non-ruminants is low. On this basis, the prohibition on processed animal protein from non-ruminants for feed for non-ruminants has been relaxed at European Union level and the resulting protein feed for aquaculture animals is subsequently authorised. Since large ships are turning slowly, this 2007 finding has not been able to amend the feed law until 2013. We ourselves comfort ourselves that we are harmonising our laws with European Union legislation. It can only be assumed that there has been economic damage to our common agriculture and that of the European Union, when we have been heating cement plant furnaces with animal protein for years. And it hasn't been the right heating either, rather the utilisation of animal protein. The European Parliament resolution of 8 March 2011 on a protein shortage and possible solutions to this long-term problem in the European Union proposes to the European Commission to allow the use of processed animal protein from slaughterhouse waste for the production of feed for monogastric animals, such as pigs and poultry, provided that the ingredients originate from meat authorised for human consumption and that the intra-species recycling and the prohibition of forced cannibalism are fully implemented and controlled. Currently, no validated diagnostic method is available to detect the presence of raw materials from pigs and poultry in feed. It is therefore not yet possible to verify the correct implementation of the intra-species recycling ban if the use of processed animal protein derived from pigs in feed for poultry and processed animal protein derived from poultry in feed for pigs is re-authorised. There are no problems with the implementation of the intra-species recycling ban in the production of aquaculture products, as the current guidance requirements for the use of fishmeal in feed for aquaculture animals have already proved effective. Therefore, feed of protein derived from non-ruminants is also authorised for aquaculture animals under certain conditions from 1 June 2013. Although this is a relatively simple draft, the government submitted it to the Riigikogu in a flawed manner. Fortunately, the good advisers of the Committee on Rural Affairs have noted that an error in the definition of animal protein in Section 6 (7) of the feed law has occurred in the draft text at point 1 of the draft. According to the original text of the draft act, animal protein is a product referred to in Annex IV, Chapter I (b) (1) (5) and Chapter II (b) (1) (3) to Regulation No 999/2001 of the European Parliament and of the Council. The products referred to in Annex IV, Chapter II (b) (1) to Regulation No 999/2001 are the same as those referred to in Annex IV, Chapter I (b) (1) (5), i.e. processed animal proteins, collagen and gelatine derived from non-ruminants, blood products, hydrolysed protein of animal origin, dicalcium phosphate of animal origin and tricalcium phosphate. Animal proteins such as milk, milk-based products, dairy products, colostrum, colostrum, egg products, egg products, collagen and gelatine derived from non-ruminants are mentioned in Chapter II (a) (1) (3) of the same Annex. It is therefore correct to refer to Chapter II (a) (1) and (3) instead of the second reference in the definition of animal protein. Now that such a fundamental error has been corrected, the Group of the Social Democratic Party agrees to support draft 431 at third reading and we will vote in favour of its adoption. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U353	The next item is the third reading of the draft Museum Act 374 initiated by the Government of the Republic. Do the groups want to open negotiations? I would like to ask my colleague, Mr Aadu, as a representative of the Black Centre Party Group.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U354	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Museums play a greater role in our society than we usually imagine. First of all, museums are the keepers of the historical memory of our people, the collectors of this memory material, the advertisers. Through this museums are also the designers of the culture and identity of our people. Another important point we could bear in mind is that museums serve the purpose of maintaining the continuity of generations. In addition, museums have taken on a major role in teaching. Today, more and more are said that the museum is an organic continuation of the school. The law is that the museum is also an educational institution. For example, the "Museum and Archive as a Learning Environment" project is known. The regulations that have so far shaped the work of museums have become hopelessly obsolete and are in the way of time. The new law was badly needed. This draft, which was now submitted for adoption, had at the beginning a number of contentions, perhaps also shortcomings. That original bill was the face of the ministry rather than the face of the museum. The draft law was designed to make it easier to boss and rule museums, not based on the purpose that the people would be interested in going there and receiving more instruction. At the same time, however, there was a sympathetic way of dealing with the draft. There was a constructive debate during the process, and new solutions were sought. For this, the Commission as a whole, and especially the leading judge Liisa-Ly Pakosta, should be congratulated, who recommended and managed to do a great deal of work. He organized a series of meetings with interest groups and specialists, with cultural people at all. And the surprise-surprisingly, when the draft museum law was finally completed, the opposition's proposals had also been harmonically incorporated into it, which happens relatively rarely in this house. It's a good bill. Maybe it sounds paradoxical, but it's also good that he's not 'too good'. If we monitor the development of the'memory institutions' in recent decades, we will see that this development has been enormously rapid. While it has been said earlier that, for example, library, archival, museology and heritage conservation are all very special and specific disciplines, the common part of which is about 20% and the special part of everyone 80%, the development of information technology has now led to the conclusion that the common part of memory sciences already accounts for about 80% and that the special part is within 20%. Perhaps there might have been the idea that now, in the museum law, we could already consider heritology, which is the current adaptation of the word heritology, not just museums. That's where the excitement begins. Today, universities are also going from teaching separate museology, archival, library and heritage conservation to teaching herthology as a discipline involving all of them. Three more minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U355	Three minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U356	Against this background, it would be expected that the museum law should also summarise all these areas. The healthy logic, however, says that the compromises made in drafting the draft were reasonable. Let the universities go first. Let them train specialists ready for tomorrow, and once these specialists are trained, it will be possible to break any professional barriers more successfully and go from one, another, third or fourth narrowly defined field of memory to a larger and more coherent direction. In summary, I would like to say that Bill 374 is good because it meets the current requirements of Estonia. Secondly, it is good that there are marked directions in the course of the proceedings, which must be taken forward. It's not like the law's finished and life stops. On the contrary, the Commission's work has shown that it is possible to move forward constructively in this area. All in all, on behalf of the Centre Party Group, I thank those who have done so for their consensus. This bill deserves to be voted in favour. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U358	Madam President! Ladies and gentlemen, It is quite good for me, after Mr Black, to continue speaking, because indeed, it has been a good example to deal with the draft law on museums. Its proceedings started right before the text was handed over to Parliament. The meeting of the Committee on Culture was held at the Museum of Estonian History in Maarjamäe, where the Minister of Culture introduced the main directions of the draft, thus testing the circulation of his ideas in the eyes of the members of the Committee on Culture. A number of major museum leaders and members of the museum council also took part in this free and open discussion. Thus, a constructive foundation was created for the draft. This does not mean that the subsequent formal proceedings went in any way particularly smooth or formal. On the contrary, as a result of the discussions in the Commission, quite a lot of changes were made to the structure of the draft, the definition of the museum was introduced and other important concepts were clarified, the emphasis of the draft was changed, and a new topic was even added on the proposal of the museum council: the possibility of disposing of the museum collection in public auction when the museum was excluded from the museum collection. Very exciting topic! What, then, could be highlighted by the content of the museum law? Couple of marginal notes on that. It is certainly important that the Commission included a definition of a museum in the draft, which also gives the museum the dimension of an educational institution with all its implications. And that's quite a lot, up to a tax credit for those people who attend a paid training course at a museum. On this subject, we even had a small dispute with the Ministry, which considered the definition to be be belletristian rather than a legal definition that could be useful in everyday life. But make sure it's the only quote in my report. Paragraph 2 (1) of the Museums Act states: "The museum is a cultural and educational institution operating permanently in the service of society and its development, which does not pursue economic profits that are open to the public and whose task is to collect, preserve, explore and mediate the cultural and cultural heritage of a person and his or her living environment for educational, scientific and experience purposes. In carrying out its tasks, the museum will, inter alia, take into account the needs of children and people with disabilities. ' This was the definition that the Committee on Culture included in the draft. Whether belletristika or not, there is also one of the most general problems of Estonian lawmaking, the immense deepening of which may increasingly narrow the room for free thought and common sense in Estonia. I mean, the attitude of what's not written, it doesn't exist. Common sense understands that the museum has an educational function, but if we had not written it in black and white, we would, unfortunately, have limited the possibilities for museums to operate and probably reduced the number of participants in museum activities. Unfortunately, yes, the principle 'not written, does not exist' is still more pragmatic than common sense because of circumstances or perhaps lack of will. The Commission made a substantial derogation when defining the conditions for applying for operating grants from the State. In the original version of the bill, the condition for applying for the grant was the accession of any type of museum to the electronic database of museums in MuIS. Joining the database would currently be too much for many non-state museums, all the more so since MuIS itself, in itself a valuable online work and exhibition tool, is still in the active development phase. Three more minutes, please!

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U360	The Ministry had given MuIS too much of a decisive role, and several museum people saw it as a lever for the extinction of smaller museums. ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U362	The second reading of the draft decision of the Riigikogu, submitted by the Government of the Republic, entitled "Use of the Defence Forces in fulfilling the international obligations of the Estonian State in the European Union Training Mission and the United Nations Peace Mission in Mali." Mr Aivar Riisalu, vice-chairman of the National Defence Committee, would you like to give a presentation?
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U363	Good President! Ladies and gentlemen, The first reading of the draft decision was completed on 6 June 2013. The deadline for tabling amendments was 12 June at 5.15 p.m. No proposal to amend the draft was received. The text of the first reading was already correct in linguistic and technical terms, so the text of the second reading is identical to the text of the first reading. The National Defence Committee discussed the draft mission for Mali before the second reading at its meeting on 13 June and 17 June. At the meeting on 17 June, it was decided by consensus to approve the explanatory memorandum and the text of the second reading of the draft and to propose to the Riigikogu Board that the draft decision be included on the agenda for the second reading of the session on 19 June. As a member of the European Union, it is important for Estonia to contribute to the implementation of the common security and defence policy of the European Union. This is also the case, in accordance with point 1 of the draft, for participation in the European Union Training Mission (EUTM Mali) with up to eight Defence Forces. Estonian Defence Forces are not involved in military operations, but are involved in training and advising the Malian Armed Forces. According to point 2 of the draft, up to two staff officers will be deployed on the United Nations peacekeeping mission MINUSMA. MINUSMA is both a traditional peacekeeping operation and the fight against terrorism. Officially, staff officers do not participate directly in military operations, but organise the activities of UN peacekeepers, including African military personnel and Mali police units. For example, Estonia's allies Finland and Norway have announced their participation in the UN mission. As a member of the United Nations, Estonia contributes to peace in crisis regions. On 13 June 2013, on the basis of § 109 of the Riigikogu Law on Home and Working Procedures, the National Defence Committee decided by consensus to propose to the Riigikogu to terminate the second reading of the draft decision of the Riigikogu, to put the draft to a final vote and to adopt it as a decision of the Riigikogu. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U371	Dear waitress, I understand that, for the first time in history, our defence forces are taking action on African soil. Now, if I understand correctly, the mission we are participating in is in the service of the legitimate government of Mali or is at least working in cooperation with it on Mali's territory. Can you give us an overview of the state of affairs there? To what extent is Mali's legitimate government currently controlling its territory, and how well is the success of this mission guaranteed? Maybe one day we'll have to get out of there in bad shape with the others.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U372	The situation in Mali is under control. In Mali, a military coup took place some time ago, to go briefly to the beginning of the problem. Mali is in this sense an unusual country that has an area of 1.5 million square kilometres. The southern part, which is below the Saharan desert, is predominantly inhabited by black people, but Mali's desert is a Tuareg area and the Tuaregs are known to be of Arabic origin. Thus, in some ways, this country has been programmed into ethnic conflict. Tuaregs, with three groups, seek autonomy or total independence in one way or another. The situation has also begun to be exploited by the Islamic fundamentalists, and at one point there was a risk that the situation might get out of control because the government no longer had a substantive say in the Tuareg territory. At that, France intervened with military force and stabilized the situation, purely at its own risk. To date, active combat activities have ended and the international community has decided that a United Nations peacekeeping unit of 12 500 men based on African states will be located in Mali. France has already taken a large part of its units out of there and the situation is stabilised by means of peacekeeping, which is why we have a reasonable contribution to make. Mali's mission is like a two-layered one for us: it is a European Union training mission, to which we send up to eight members of the Defence Forces, at the moment of rotation they are double. They are specifically involved in the training of the Malian army. Two members of the Defence Forces under the UN mandate perform a wide range of tasks in the United Nations mission. In this sense, it is a unique condition, which is usually a NATO mission, a European Union mission or a UN mission, but at present, two acceptable forces throughout the world have taken control of the regulation of the situation. I believe that this will keep the situation stable and the crisis will not escalate. In the first step, people in Estonia were tried to explain to us that why Africa and all of this are not right for us. I don't think Mali will come from another Afghanistan, for the simple reason that they got it in time. Let's just say that we support the legitimate government as far as, in the case of Africa, we can talk about a legitimate government at all. To be honest, we support a government that has come to power with a coup, but it is in power. I think a better solution, as has now been achieved there, than the fact that there is going to be some sort of crisis, in which we would pretend that it does not concern us. One and a half million square kilometers is a very large area, and there are not many people living there, so it's possible to deliver anyone in this area, even the bad guys, if they just want to. That's why I think we're walking the right path.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U449	Let's move on to item nine. The second reading of the basic school and upper secondary school law initiated by the Government of the Republic and the related draft law amending other laws begins. I ask for a presentation of the Chair of the Riigikogu, Chairman of the Cultural Commission Urmas Klaas!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U450	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The Committee on Culture of the Riigikogu has prepared for a second reading a list of texts and explanatory notes and amendments to the basic school and upper secondary school law and related amendments to other laws. The Government of the Republic initiated this draft on 13 December 2012. The draft is based on the principles of modernisation of the educational organisation developed by the Ministry of Education and Research. The aim is to better ensure that quality education is equally accessible and to change the forms of basic school and upper secondary school operations, the organisation of teaching, the management of the school, the work of teachers, their remuneration and the principles of financing the school. On the proposal of the Committee on Culture, the Management Board of the Riigikogu set the deadline for submitting amendments on 15 March. Amendments to the draft were tabled by the Estonian Centre Party Group and the Social Democratic Party Group. A total of 29 institutions, associations and people, from the Association of Estonian Early Education Leaders to the Vallavolikogu of Tõstamaa and Tõstamaa Municipality and Tõstamaa Secondary School, submitted their proposals and opinions. The Committee on Culture discussed the draft, preparing it for a second reading, at 26 sessions. The Commission divided the draft into thematic blocks and discussed these issues with stakeholders. Following the examination of the thematic blocks, the amendments were examined and voted through. There were over 200 of them. Today, ladies and gentlemen, 92 amendments have been tabled in the list of amendments. The Committee on Culture has taken procedural decisions on the draft, which will be proposed to a large hall. On 13 June we decided to propose that Bill 340 should come to second reading in Riigikogu plenary on 19 June. On 18 June, we decided to propose the end of the second reading of the draft. When the second reading is completed, the third reading of the draft should be placed on the agenda of the additional sitting of the Riigikogu as soon as possible. I will now give more details on the amendments. I'll deal with them in the thematic blocks, so you'll find it easier to follow what I'm saying. The first amendment has come from the Group of the Estonian Centre Party and concerns the abandonment of the concept of pedagogy provided for in the draft. The Ministry of Education and Research has confirmed that the draft is only a replacement of one concept. In the draft, the term 'pedagog' is replaced by 'Director', 'director','support specialist' and 'teacher'. This step does not reduce the guarantees of any person to date. The Committee on Culture did not support this proposal of the Centre Party. It is worth noting, however, that the Commission considered it necessary to further analyse the issue of teacher assistance as a whole, also with a view to supporting the development of the student. Amendments 2 to 10 deal with the forms of school operation. The Committee on Culture did not support the amendments proposed by the Central Party Group and the Social Democratic Party Group concerning the forms of school operation. It was proposed to delete amendments to the forms of school operation, together with the implementing provisions, from the draft. As a result of the Commission's discussion, the Ministry of Education and Research proposed in the draft to clarify this, it is important that if we want to reconcile education of interest with general education, allowing general education to also provide an education of interest in general education, students who are studying under an area-of-interest curriculum must also be guaranteed the rights they have when studying in a separate community-of-interest school. The funding of an education of interest must not give rise to differences depending on whether the school of interest is acting as a separate institution or together with the general school as a joint institution. I consider it important to mention Amendments 16 and 18. They relate to the obligation of the three courses of study in upper secondary school. In particular, the Committee on Culture proposes to remove from the draft the obligation for upper secondary schools to offer students three courses of study. In essence, the Committee on Culture has taken into account the proposals of the Estonian Centre Party in this regard. The draft did not initially intend to address this issue, but the amendments allow it. It is important to say that, although, as a result of the discussion in the Committee on Culture, we decided to exclude the obligation of the three courses of study, as it has become an artificial requirement, there is a provision in the draft which obliges the upper secondary school to provide students with sufficient choice, as is appropriate for the upper secondary school. It is important to note that the issue of Steiner and the Waldorf schools has also resolved by formulating this amendment. They were concerned that if three courses of study were rigidly required, then the specific curriculum, pedagogical content and keeping of these schools at all could be compromised. Proposals Nos 19 and 20 concern the obligation of the State to hold upper secondary school. We did not support the Central Party Group's proposal that the state should agree with the local government to ensure the protection of the interests of the local community when setting up a secondary school. This problem has been solved in such a way that the state commits itself to more clearly ensuring access to high-quality upper secondary school education. The draft requires at least one state-owned upper secondary school in each county by 2020. This is something that the Ministry of Education and Research would certainly like to mention here, that the school network is wanted to be organized, of course, in cooperation with municipalities and on the basis of mutual agreements. There was also a question of whether in the future it would be possible to consider that the entire upper secondary school was held at national level. The Ministry replied that this could not be ruled out. Proposals Nos 21 and 23 concern ensuring compliance with school obligations. Once again, both the amendment on the three courses of study and the amendment on ensuring compliance with school obligations were made in consensus within the Commission. The Committee on Culture proposes that the proposed changes to the conditions for determining the place of residence be removed from the draft. The proposal of the Ministry of Education was not supported by the Commission, and we are now continuing with the existing regulation of the PGS on the place of residence school, under which every child must be provided with a place in the school of his place of residence. If there is room in the school, students can also be taken from outside the area. I believe this is a customary arrangement for many. This draft does not deal with school tests; we did not change this regulation. Amendment 24 concerns parental co-financing when a child is studying under an international curriculum. There was also a very long and in-depth discussion with stakeholders on this issue. The Committee on Culture proposes to withdraw from the draft the possibility proposed by the government that schools may ask parents for co-financing if the child is studying on the basis of an international curriculum. This is an IBO curriculum. The Commission found that there has not been sufficient analysis of the situation where pupils in general education have paid for their studies and students who study free of charge. This was not the case with the IBO curriculum. The Commission's proposal is the proposed option to delete it from the draft. Proposal No 26 concerns teaching literature. I would like to say that there was a very in-depth debate in the Commission on several occasions with representatives of publishers. The Commission asked the Ministry of Education and Research to draw up a proposal to amend the draft. We approved this proposal, which was presented to us by the Ministry, which has been taken into account as a Commission proposal. According to this, the Minister is in any case empowered to establish a minimum list of teaching literature and therefore the State must ensure that there is a minimum level of teaching literature, but this list is not mandatory for schools to choose teaching literature. I can explain it later, but that's the way it is. Secondly, the proposal provides (there has also been a great deal of debate about this) that the problem of access to digital teaching literature can be resolved under the conditions and procedures agreed between the parties, i.e. the Ministry and the publisher. It's not like the publishing house has to do what the ministry says. Negotiations are taking place and a clear agreement needs to be reached. Proposals 28 and 29 concern school holidays. This issue was not initially included in the draft, but on the proposal of our colleagues from the Estonian Centre Party Group, we dealt with it thoroughly. The idea is that schools, both in primary schools and in upper secondary schools, have the opportunity and the right not to be obliged, but to have the possibility and right to impose an additional break. We are talking about spring when students may be exhausted because of the long winter and the rainy summer that preceded it. Then there's an extra break, but it's only an option. Amendments 32 and 33 concern the conditions for admission to upper secondary school. No major changes have been made here, there are some clarifications. Amendments 36 and 37 on graduating from upper secondary school should be discussed in greater detail. The Committee on Culture proposes to amend the conditions for graduating from upper secondary school, which will enter into force on 1 September 2013. With the PGS that came into force in 2010, we introduced a requirement that a threshold of 50% should be set for the positive certification of state examinations. This has been a major response in society. The Ministry of Education came up with a proposal to change the state examination system and to return essentially to earlier arrangements, as they were introduced in 1996. More importantly, we have three state exams, but the result of the state exams is not directly binding on the student, I stress. The State Exam shall be deemed to have been passed if 1% of the points have been collected. Proposals 38 and 42 are extremely important. They concern access to support services and support for student development. In short, on the proposal of the Committee on Culture, the Ministry of Education and Research prepared amendments to ensure better access to support services, i.e. specialised pedagogy, social pedagogy and psychologist services. A legal basis will be created for the establishment of regional support centres, which will certainly significantly improve the availability of these services. It is important that the Minister of Education and Research can be obliged by regulation to establish a description of the support services and the scope, conditions and procedures for their provision by the State. I believe that this is a very important change. Proposal No 39 concerns development discussions. This is a very long debate. It remains the procedure for the development discussions to take place. I would also like to mention as important the proposal No 54, with which we will significantly increase the role of schools' chambers of care. It was a proposal from the Parents' Association. The board of trustees has a role to play, inter alia, in coordinating the organisation of teachers' salaries. Proposals No 55.60 concern school staff. As a result of the discussion by the Committee on Culture, the Ministry of Education and Research prepared a new and more precise wording concerning school staff, based on the wording of the VET Act. The question is that from 1 January 2014 a switch to the professional system will take place. This system does not directly replace the system of accreditation, as these systems have different objectives, but the qualifications of teachers will take effect. It is important to say that the higher levels of accreditation are valid until the end of their nominal period. The payment of remuneration for grades is equally guaranteed. Proposals No 61 was made on teacher's pay. The Commission did not support the amendments tabled by the Estonian Centre Party Group and the Social Democratic Party Group. However, we have supplemented the bill by the need for the headmaster to negotiate remuneration arrangements with teachers and to listen to the board of trustees and submit them to the school manager for approval. In the past, the Director had a very high degree of exclusive competence, and now these coordination steps have been introduced here. Proposals No 69-78 on the financing of schools are very important. The Committee on Culture proposes to remove the proposed changes to funding from the draft. It is important to add what we have also written in the explanatory memorandum: the Ministry of Education and Research has promised us that in autumn, when a new draft PGS on funding comes up, there will be a financing regulation. Prior to the discussions in the Riigikogu, the funding arrangements so far will not be amended by the Government of the Republic regulation. It is important that amendments to the Pre-school Children's Institution Act have been taken out of here, as the Ministry is planning to come up with a separate draft on pre-school children's institutions. The proposals have now been substantially examined. I have already given an overview of the procedural decisions. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U464	Thank you. ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U639	Ladies and gentlemen, Many of you may ask why a professional policeman takes the floor during the discussion of the law on basic school and upper secondary school, but I will simply remind you, to refresh your memory, that I too have had the honour of being a school principal for nearly five years, or four cupcakes. I ran a very specific school, police college. I was also a member of the European Police College Council. Why am I talking about this? For a very simple reason. It was only recently in the Committee on Legal Affairs that we drafted an opinion on the European Commission's proposal to abolish the European Police College as it is today and to bring it all together in one place, in Brussels and in The Hague. We all opposed this because the principle of the functioning of this system so far has been regional, i.e. all the Member States have been able to contribute. Exactly the money has also been distributed. Teachers and others have been satisfied. This is where I get to double standards. On the one hand, our attitude to the centralising requirements of the European Union, and on the other hand, we will do everything to ensure that Estonia's education policy runs counter to the same standard that we want to get from Europe. What am I saying? The PGS, which we are discussing today, has a much broader meaning than regulation in one area. With this law, we indirectly organise regional and social policy, but also all other policies, such as budgetary policy. You can always ask how and why. We are talking about a very specific subject, we are talking about education. But in fact, this indirect arrangement is very simple. First of all, we're pushing a lot of local schools towards closing down. Small and so-called outlying local authorities are unable to perform all the functions that we have foreseen in the PGS draft: maintaining a schoolhouse, having a qualified frame, not to mention what a good parent would expect of a school in the interests of his child. We're essentially taking away the buffer from the school for additional functions. It is clear that if there is a state gymnasium, children will move there as well. My parents want to do better for them. I now come from regional policy to social policy. We now see in Estonia that more and more people are going to work abroad. As we assemble the children in the centers, the question arises as to whether these children will ever return to where they once left. We will certainly also break families, create tensions and ultimately work against our country's primary objective of maintaining our nation and country. If people feel that the state doesn't care about them, education is one aspect of this caring, then they do not feel the need to contribute to the country either. All of this will have an indirect impact on future fiscal policy. Soon we will be seriously concerned about where our taxpayers are, and we will be talking more and more about whether migration policy is right or wrong, while doing everything at the same time to make it as negative as possible for our own people. As a lawyer, I will conclude with one point, on which the chairman of the committee also said that things are not very well here. This concerns the wording of the draft law. I asked in the round of questions what the foundation of the state was all about. I didn't get a very clear answer. We no longer have public limited liability companies, we have public limited liability companies and we also have foundations. It is a legal entity governed by private law, in which the State has a part. We are now introducing a new concept. Moreover, there are still a great many shortcomings in the wording. A good example is § 71: 'A schoolkeeper ensures... ' That's not a good wording. Language editors have not seen this bill. But it must be taken into account that this law affects tens of thousands of people. Why, then, do we write the law so complicated that a simple person, a parent, or a teacher who doesn't exactly teach law doesn't understand it? All this hastens and reminds us of the draft law amending the Land Tax Act, which was discussed in this Chamber a few years ago, which did not suffer any criticism in terms of wording, because it was incomprehensible, unclear and raised questions. The same is true of this bill, which is much more extensive and on which very many people depend their plans and ideas for the future. What am I saying? It is not right that the final text of such a very important draft law will only be received by the members of the commission in the dusk of last night. People look at what we are doing in this Chamber and decide whether they pay their taxes in Estonia or are in the vicinity of another country that actually cares about its citizens. Thank you.

Unfortunately, their opinion has not actually been taken into account, and any talk about good legislative practice and inclusion is not simply sincere in this draft. The concept of an assistant teacher or teacher's help at school also disappears. Looks like the minister's read too much of Oskar Luts' Springs. ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U692	Ladies and gentlemen, In the last few days, I've had a lot of talk with teachers and school principals. I would like to address the issues and issues that were the most acutely discussed in these negotiations here on the board today. The first major block to be discussed is teacher training. Paragraph 75 (2) of the draft law on basic school and upper secondary school states that the professional duty of a teacher is to supplement his professional skills and to keep up with educational innovations. In my opinion, before we demand anything, we need to create opportunities to meet this requirement. It's a pretty complicated situation right now. Firstly, the Ministry of Education and Research and the national allocation of 2%, the more the 1% to come will not be enough to train a qualified and qualified teacher. The Ministry of Education allows free training that is currently organised by universities. These are not mostly topical on the subject, are short-term or are too low in the number of study places, and unfortunately not all those wishing to get there will be able to get there. Another serious concern is that the country has no vision of the teacher training system as a whole, nor has a solid training strategy been created. Currently, schools can only choose from a rather limited and uncoordinated range of opportunities offered by training companies and universities. Courses often repeat each other, topics overlap. Lectors may not even know about school life. The knowledge acquired in chaoticly in their subject (some of the necessary subjects remain overlooked by trainers) does not help the teacher but create even more confusion. Thirdly, I would also like to say a few words for the protection of Russian-speaking teachers. The transition to Estonian-language learning has been well supported by the efforts of the state and trainings that support it are ongoing. But let us not forget that in Russian-language schools most subjects are still taught in Russian. Teachers working in these schools deserve as much attention as their Estonian-speaking colleagues, and they also need training. For example, the Russian language and literature teachers of Kohtla-Järve schools recently complained that they had not had any practical training for seven years. How can we require teachers to have systematic knowledge and a comprehensive life picture if the state does not guarantee this at teacher level? The second block is state examinations and graduating from upper secondary school. Paragraph 31 (5) of the Basic School and Upper Secondary Schools Act currently provides that the state examination has been passed satisfactorily if at least 50% of the maximum result has been achieved. Now that the test is over, we can talk about how real it is to meet this requirement in its current form. Unfortunately, schools had to admit that a threshold of 50% would not allow many 11th grade students to complete upper secondary school. 20% was a viable and reasonable requirement that motivated students to make an effort to graduate, but did not panic them. Teachers and school leaders are depressed about this situation. It must not be forgotten that any good child may be talented in mathematics, but foreign languages are not progressing in any way. If we keep the 50% barrier, our universities may lose talented students and winners of the Olympiads because they lose the opportunity to graduate from upper secondary school and receive secondary education. The third block I want to talk about today is the support granted by the state. Paragraph 1 (46) of the draft law states that special provisions are to be allocated annually from the state budget to municipalities and cities. Unfortunately, this provision excluded where this money could be allocated and should be allocated, namely for the remuneration of support specialists, specialised pedagogues, psychologists, logopeds and others. Thus, it is up to the local authorities to have these specialists in schools. If the local government has enough money, schools can provide support systems and support specialists. Local authorities differ in their material base, but children with special educational needs continue everywhere and their number continues to increase. Given that the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act provides for the establishment of special classes and groups in ordinary schools, the Director is also obliged to ensure the availability of these specialists. It is logical that the law provides for the obligation of the State to pay the fees to support specialists. The preparation and further training of support specialists will also be a painful place. § 44 of the draft law states that the Minister of Education and Research establishes qualification requirements for support specialists. In order to meet the qualification requirements, support professionals must undergo training. The state has allocated 1% for training, which is intended only to meet the training needs of teachers. The amount allocated from the local government budget for training is often small and does not cover the training needs of support specialists. It would be reasonable to provide in the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act state support for the further training of support specialists. We hope that this issue will be resolved in the autumn, as has already been said several times on this board. Finally, it is already difficult for schools to monitor the ever-changing changes in the PGS, with educational reforms coming one after the other not providing an opportunity to assess the effectiveness of the previous reform and its impact on the education system. Instead of real work, teaching students, school leaders and teachers need to constantly monitor changes and the avalanche of papers requiring further changes is already moving towards the school. Unfortunately, in this situation, these changes to the PGS cannot be supported. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U698	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Several class managers have claimed that up to 80% of parents do not answer their e-mails, receive parents on a mobile phone at night, and then have long conversations about their successor's behavior. You don't usually hang up. In the future, do the class managers have to start paying these phone bills out of their already small teacher's salary? At the same time, it has been established by law that there must be development discussions, and the class manager must also organise events that unite the class and support children's development. There should be a minimum fee for class management, from which the school could pay a higher fee, or a minimum fee for a single development interview. In the future, the integration of students of other nationalities will be added to this, so the work of the head of class will increase in the future. Teachers will have a 35-hour working week. It has not been established how much of the teacher's overall working time constitutes direct education and education, i.e. giving an hour. The regime so far, which provides 18 to 24 hours of substance, is unfortunately abolished. From now on, the teacher will face a much higher workload, as after the abolition of the load limits, the teacher can also send the teacher to the class for 35 hours, plus the time to prepare the hours. But if the teacher's still in charge of the class! Three exclamations and three question marks should be placed at the end of this sentence. According to the draft law, the levels of qualification of teachers will disappear. Thus, teachers are placed in a single grade, which in turn does not motivate teachers to constantly supplement themselves. In addition, this means that the salaries of teachers will be increased at the expense of the salaries of more experienced teachers. Today, a Methodist earns over a hundred euros more than a normal teacher. Thus, because of the alleged increase in the pay of teachers organised by Minister Aaviksoo, the Methodist loses his real income, which may lead to the leaving of the school for specialists. It also reduces teachers' motivation to do additional work, for example, to draw up workbooks, which are now needed to gain a career as a Methodist. In financing local authorities, the state is now earmarked to allocate funds only to the minimum wage of teachers. No funds are allocated to the head of study, textbooks, workbooks and other educational materials such as brushes, balls, etc., no money is foreseen. In addition to the means of paying the minimum wage, municipalities now need to find their own resources for all other educational expenditures, which until now were considered to be part of education support. It is necessary to find money for the recognition of more experienced teachers, to support classroom management, choir management and recreational activities, to support special children, to pay for museum visits, to support participation in regional and national events, etc. In addition to the above, support specialists are no longer funded: school psychologists, special pedagogues, logos, etc. This means that support professionals disappear from school, which in turn worsens the situation of students. The amendment is particularly cynical at the moment when the new curriculum already prescribes obligations for schoolkeepers, for which there are no resources. In addition, the Ministry of Education and Research proposes to fund schools directly bypassing local authorities, which is not in line with the European understanding of the right to self-determination of local authorities. Reading all that has been written down in the bill, I once again have a rhetorical question: what kind of extension of evil power has the Fatherland Union and his successor IRL acted over time, if they have constantly tried to push the Estonian state and people towards destruction? I will give just a few examples of this huge long list. Tens of thousands of people and families were started to be evicted from their homes under the infamous ownership reform label. There was an attempt to destroy agriculture, but fortunately there was something left of it, and it is now being restored. For the price of the sandwich, they tried to parse Estonian power plants, with the price being lower than the amount now taken out each year as a dividend. Now they want to destroy the education system of the main architect of national self-being and survival. What an evil thing to do to your people! There is no way to support this. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U700	Dear Chairman of the Riigikogu! Dear Chairman of the Board of the Estonian Federation of Educational Workers Reemo Voltri! Ladies and gentlemen, I will stop on the question of why neither I nor my entire group can support the adoption of Bill 340 as its current form, despite the fact that the controversial financing chapter was removed from the draft, and the draft has also made positive changes on the initiative of the Commission, which had not even been proposed by the Ministry of Education. Looking at the remaining parts of the draft, it is a pity that the Ministry, and therefore the Coalition, did not support any of our suggestions for improvement, even though they did not contradict the positions of the Ministry of Education, but were simply trying to make the provisions of the draft more rational and acceptable to municipalities and educators. I will introduce you to these proposals in a more fundamental way. The desire of the Ministry to establish state secondary schools in the county towns and its current activities in this regard are very good and welcome. But why does this legally exclude the possibility of setting up full-cycle schools or progymnasiums in the future? Why should our education system not be more flexible and subject to natural competition? Why do you have to start financialally strangling those rural governments who have decided to keep upper secondary school education in their school? Why must the creation of clean upper secondary schools be practiced in essence by coercion, just as Soviet power in its time created a collective society? The Ministry once again knows what's only good and pushes it on stupid parish and school leaders. Support for Amendment 3 would be limited to the legal possibility of establishing full cycle schools, progymnasiums and a combined general education and recreational school in the future. After all, existing schools can continue to work under point 53 anyway. The possibility of financial survival in our small towns and rural settlements of high schools with a vibrant and strong tradition of 60-100 upper secondary school students should be guaranteed by the lack of funding of the draft. However, our amendment No 19, which we ask to support, would provide a substantive basis for this by clearly separating the tasks of the state and the municipality in the provision of upper secondary school education, giving the state full responsibility for the completion of the curriculum and supporting the municipality in the creation of a learning environment and the development of students. This would mean school- and curriculum-based funding for upper secondary school education rather than student funding. This is the path that Minister Kisler offered in Question Time, saying that the funding of upper secondary schools must be based on the decision made by the state and local government associations which secondary schools will remain in the county and then on equal treatment of these schools, not on unfair competition between secondary schools, which is currently being created by the Ministry with the draft submitted. The IRL could understand that the idea of administrative reform, based on attraction centres, offered by the Minister of Regional Affairs, becomes ridiculous when the Minister of Education previously destroys secondary schools in these centres of attraction. Let me give you an example. Why would Avinurmes, a high school with a strong 70 students and two well-developed courses of study, be destroyed by the chairman of a successful reform party council? Why should neighboring municipalities, sending their young people to Avinurme School, pay a bounty for them than children in a farther, richer state gymnasium in Jõhvi? After all, the Ministry's second basic idea is also good: to increase the rights and responsibilities of the head of school, especially when determining salary. Of course, the chairman of the board of the Estonian Association of School Leaders at Toomas Kruusimäe is right, who said that this option would only make sense if schools were financed on the basis of a 16-hour standard load rather than a 21-hour standard load, as has been done and will be done in the future. However, this would mean allocating additional funding to the education system, which the Minister of Education does not even apply for. However, the abolition of minimum school compositions and hourly rates currently proposed in the draft leaves the teacher completely helpless in wage negotiations. The negotiations will essentially disappear at all, because what is the point of negotiating only the minimum wage, which is dictated by the Minister of Finance anyway. I do not think that working conditions and book rules on workload should be laid down by law, but with our proposal No 61, we are making them the subject of national tripartite negotiations, while maintaining negotiations and laying the foundations for specific collective agreements in schools. By granting more rights to school leaders, a system should also be set up to enable them to be checked and replaced if necessary. We do not want fixed-term employment contracts for school leaders, which would leave them at the mercy of political attraction winds, but with proposal No 49 we propose that a system of periodic accreditation of directors should be established in accordance with the procedure laid down by the Ministry. It was completely unclear why the Ministry was opposed to this proposal as well. Of course, we are also completely unacceptable with the proposed concept of a class leader and the abolition of the class-leading fee, which would cause irreparable damage to the educational work in schools. The Ministry wishes to abolish the current procedure for the accreditation of teachers and the related grades. This is again an idea that is modern and could be supported if accreditation is replaced by a professional system. Unfortunately, the bill doesn't say a word about it. The professional system is left to the private pleasure of professional associations because the Ministry does not want to link it to the pay of teachers. In this way, we lose the motivation for self-improvement of existing teachers, without offering anything else to replace it. If we are of the opinion that it may not be necessary to link professional levels to a particular salary number, the funding of schools must also depend on the professionalism of teachers, otherwise we will have a system that simply does not work or does the opposite. By characterizing the entire bill as a whole, we can see that it was designed not to remove the substantive bottlenecks in upper secondary school education, but to ensure that the teachers' minimum wage was increased. Once again, a noble and necessary idea. However, the current minister thinks that the salaries of teachers are low in Estonia, not because the state does not give money, but because there are too many teachers and schools and because the municipalities spend education money elsewhere. It is precisely in order to combat these two evils that legislative changes have been created. The cornerstone of the ministerial ideology has become the teacher's salary, which has been torn out of the rest of the educational landscape and polished as a pillar of freedom. No one doubts that the low wages are the greatest troubles in our education system. The common sense, however, means the real salary that comes into the teacher's pocket, the aim of which is to ensure that the class is faced with motivated, authoritative professionals who are able to work with each student individually and to improve their knowledge. The Minister of Realist understands, however, the number of money written into the state budget, which he has traded from the Minister of Finance for the minimum wage of teachers and teachers only. Two pillars are foreseen to ensure this number of money. Some of the money needed is already taken from the pockets of poor municipalities and the second part is created by the organisation of the school network, which results in a thinning of the school network and a decrease in the number of teachers, but increases in the number of hours provided and in the number of pupils in schools and classes. How this will affect the development of each student's personality and the quality of school education and education is up to each listener to determine his or her life experience. I fear that, if the educational landscape is shaped like this, young people will not choose the profession of pedagogy, even if the teacher's nominal minimum wage should move closer to the average wage in the country. The purpose of this operation is to reject the responsibility and resentment for local governments and school leaders in case the teachers who perceive the lack of real wage growth start to strike next time. What are the consequences of the proposed changes to regional policy, the education system as a whole or to a specific student, the Ministry does not seem to be at all interesting. Of course, we cannot support a law built on such logic. The Estonian education system can only be helped by the actual increase in teachers' wages above the average wage of the state. This requires making education a real priority for the state budget, not just playing with words and figures and hoping that it could be increased by raising money from one pocket to another. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U724	Madam President! Ladies and gentlemen, I guess we should figure out why we're doing all this here this night. In my speech, I rely, like many previous speakers, on the positions of the Estonian Federation of Educational Workers. However, this is the largest organisation of teachers and other educational staff in Estonia, who has also addressed the members of the Riigikogu separately, in the hope that the opinion of the teachers or, by the newest definition, the directors, lecturers, teachers and support staff will be heard. According to the Estonian educational staff, the draft will bring about major and fundamental changes in the whole of education, especially for all teachers and the support and support staff of schools. Changes in the principles of remuneration, working time, duties, qualifications requirements and organisation of studies take place. There are three factors that the educational staff consider important. First of all, they consider that the teacher's rules on working time should also set a maximum limit on full-time training due to the specific nature of the teacher's work. If it is only determined that the teacher has 35 hours a week, this creates uncertainty for the teachers. They do not know how long their hours are and what their duties are. The initiators and guardians of the draft act stress the responsibility of the Director for the organisation of studies at school and the trust of the teacher and the director. At some points, it may seem that reducing the conditions laid down by law and placing greater emphasis on the decision-making power of the Director are in line with the principle of subsidiarity. However, the concept of proximity has been misused here. Proximity, or subsidiarity, does not mean that everything at local level is undefined and that the local decision-maker determines all aspects. There are certain fundamental rights, they are fundamental constitutional rights, civil rights or human rights. Such a debate has often taken place in this Chamber when there has been talk of standard-setting for public services provided by local authorities. It has been considered that the definition of certain service standards would, as if, undermine the right of municipalities to decide independently. Yeah, that's it. It will, but it is about ensuring the fundamental rights of people and the uniform availability of public services. The municipality cannot be free in its decisions here. Why am I bringing such a parallel? Because it is similar to the current situation when dealing with legislative changes. The advocates of the bill stress that the head of school must be given the opportunity to decide and we should not be afraid to give more powers to school leaders. We are not afraid of this, but the solution lies in the fact that decisions taken under restricted conditions are being pushed onto the shoulders of school managers and school leaders. There is no more comfortable situation for senior managers of the education system than if it were possible to blame the local government and the school manager for all the troubles. A school manager who sets the upper limit of lessons, the status of a class manager and his/her remuneration, and all other important and less important aspects of the school's organisation of work. However, such a strategy is not linked to the principle of subsidiarity. When we talk about a more precise definition of teacher's working time, it is more about the safety of the teaching community under the labour law. An employee-employer relationship is not left solely dependent on the employer's will and the outcome of the negotiations. If that were the case, we would have no labour laws at all. An application by educators for a more precise determination of working time can be considered, in particular, to be of employment law, to seek greater security and uncertainty in the work of the teacher. According to § 29 of the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia, the conditions of employment are controlled by the State. The emphasis on the decision-making of the Director should not make the organisation of school life any special category outside the scope of the Constitution. It is obviously not enough to set a limit on working time alone, the state should not leave teachers without the protection of labour law, and the introduction of working conditions should at least make collective bargaining a clear subject, as the Socialist proposal provides. I would also like to highlight another aspect that education workers consider important. They have pointed out that their organization strongly opposes the abolition of a separate fee for the work of a class manager. There is a certain number of basic competences that the community hopes to develop in school. These basic competences form part of a certain social order. As an educational institution, the school is known to develop three types of social quality: knowledge, skills and attitudes. Our education system offers fact-based education, we do not know enough about social competences, or we do not consider it necessary. A class manager's work in his class is one way to develop students' social competences. Especially in primary and primary school stages, a parent expects the school environment to be safe first, then to have good children raised there, and then to have smart children. This is a social subscription to the school, which, unfortunately, differs greatly from the spirit of the national curriculum. The older and more independent the child becomes, the more the parent hopes that the developing young person will receive high-quality knowledge and a good position for further studies. It is at primary and primary school level that the role of the head of the classroom is particularly important in the school. Making the position and role of a class manager merely an internal issue of the school clearly weakens the position of the school as an institution developing social competences in society. While we spoke earlier about knowledge, skills and attitudes, the changes currently being addressed will result in the school becoming a frying, highly economical and minimal body offering a knowledge package with which the community is unlikely to be satisfied. This school should not be satisfied with this composition of the Riigikogu. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U726	Madam President! Ladies and gentlemen, Today's Bill 340 has been presented at length and in depth by the Commission, with further questions on it, but there are still a lot of loose ends, questions and ambiguities. The Estonian Federation of Educational Workers, the largest organisation that unites teachers and other educational staff in Estonia, has indeed turned to all of us 101 members of the public. They have highlighted the following concerns and principles. The draft will bring about major and fundamental changes in the whole of education. This is particularly true for all teachers and school support and support staff. Their remuneration principles, working hours, duties, qualifications requirements and organisation of studies will be changed. Now I quote: 'We consider it important to emphasise our views once again. 1. We continue to believe that the teacher's working time regulation should also include a maximum limit on full-time learning due to the specifics of teacher work. The definition of teacher working time in just 35 hours per week creates uncertainty for teachers in the definition of working time and duties. We believe that there should be a national upper limit for full-time teachers (18 lessons per week on the EHL proposal, 16 lessons per week for composite classes) to avoid teacher overwork and a fall in the quality of the whole learning process. This amendment, which would no longer set a limit on lessons, puts the teacher at risk of having to do more work for the same money, and in worse cases, for example, instead of a maximum of 24 lessons so far, the teacher will have to give 30 hours, plus, of course, the time to improve the preparation and the work, etc. There's no way it can fit into 35 hours of general work. Studies carried out by the EHL show that the teacher is already working more than 50 hours a week if the teacher can teach up to a maximum of 24 lessons. 2. The teacher shall be provided with a salary corresponding to the general period, which shall include the performance of all the duties. Only one minimum rate of pay is set at national level. The teacher's monthly earnings are also currently covered by the current basic school and upper secondary school law and the Employment Contracts Act, for all the work done by the teacher, which, on a full-time basis, the teacher has 35 hours per week [---]. At present, the Government of the Republic Regulation sets minimum rates of pay for 4 teachers nationwide according to the grades awarded to the teacher for the assessment of qualifications and, separately, the minimum rates for class management fees. The amendments retain only one minimum rate of pay covering both class management fees and other tasks. The EHL is opposed to the introduction of only one minimum rate of pay, as it does not motivate teachers to develop themselves and apply for higher qualifications and grades. 3. We strongly oppose the abolition of the separate remuneration for the work of the head of class. The amendments to the PGS are intended to abolish a separate fee for the performance of the duties of the class manager. This step is dangerous given the importance of the role of class manager in the social development of our young people, support for learning, etc. ' The Educational Workers' Association hopes that the members of the Riigikogu will listen and respect these principles. That's why I thought it was necessary to present them here. We have talked a lot about teachers' work, less about students. The National Audit Office's report pointed out that only 85 municipalities have child protection workers who are already unable to do this work. The proposal also provides for the inclusion of the tasks of a social pedagogue for a child welfare worker or a social worker. This is definitely not the solution. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U728	Dear Chairman, Ladies and gentlemen, Already 40 years ago, as a young schoolteacher, a simple wisdom was taught: The lesson must be given in such a way that the listeners are able to accept what is being offered. A longtime rector of the University of Tartu, our recent colleague in the Committee on Culture, means that a colleague from the previous season Peeter Tulviste once spoke about educational reform, telling a story about two villagers. The father and son discussed how to repair the thin roof of their broken cynic hut. The son said: "You know, Father, today the sun shines, it is warm and dry, which we will repair from the empty one. " At that, Father said: " But tomorrow it will rain suddenly, and it will be bad to fix the roof in the rain. " So they discussed the matter. The moral of the story is that it is not worth leaving education reform without rain or in bad times. I am now translating this parable into a common Estonian language: changing the PGS has not fallen into a good time. Old wisdom says that any reform, even one designed simply to save a lot of money in the future, still requires money. If there is no money and there is no reform without the necessary tools to repair the roof, then it happens that good ideas can be wasted and made a laughing stock, but then the culprit will be found who has made such a stupid reform. With this reform, we have reached repairing the roof in rainy weather. Our PGS is actually worse than the villagers' cynic roof. In addition, we have a neighbour who promised to cut the chips for roof repair because, you know, the roof cannot be repaired if there is no material. The neighbor said he'd bring those chips in with his horse. But now I don't even know if he meant cutting chips to cut them or what he meant. It is explained that this means a strange situation in which the same basic school and upper secondary school law has started to organise the school network without anyone being able to do more with the administrative reform than talk nicely about it. This is the second time this has happened. During the previous composition of the Riigikogu, the administrative reform was also attempted in the framework of the PGS. Maybe the Ministry of Education and Research is so brave and can do it. This is, of course, a very difficult task. However, it may happen that the school network is suddenly getting somewhere, at least in the way it suits the ministry, but the rest of the country and society are not at all satisfied with this administrative reform. Now for the funding. The separation of funding from the rest of the law has been done quite several times here. It could be spoken in this same figurative language, speaking of education as a burden on a wagon and a proud horse carrying it. But now it's like it's halfway through education. The cart and the load are still there, but the horse is no longer ahead, and we are planning what to put on a cart without a horse and how that cart should move. But we don't know until autumn what kind of a pig gets in the way and how much weight he can carry. Maybe there'll be a good Tori stallion, maybe a goat or a kangaroo, but maybe a camel or the same elephant that died in Narva. So we are in a situation where it is not clear how much resources there are when talking about the fees for class management or anything else. It is not clear what the role of local government is. It has been said repeatedly here that this role is likely to be very large and is too much for the municipality. It is not known what everything needs to be financed from the 20% that had to be the incentive fund that the director can use. We don't know how much and what there's enough money for. Maybe the director will finally have to pay for God knows what specialists and other fees. Even now, Tartu and Tallinn's schoolmasters are concerned that this 20% going to the incentive fund does not really mean that their real wages will fall. Of course, there are many problems and, fortunately, they have been mentioned. There have been talks about school areas, class management fees and support services, about different aspects of educational life. I want to talk about publishing textbooks, too. Today, a publisher asked me if it would be wise not to take action in this area. What's this about? He explained that the reason was the requirement that educational materials should also be placed on the Ministry's portal. Illustrations licences are then more expensive than paper-based publications, etc. I see the clock is ticking and the evening hour is late. I won't tell the fairy tale anymore. In times of crisis, solidarity societies have been able to provide their people with subsistence and assistance of any kind. I believe that, before progress is made with the draft, any proposed changes, including their possible impact, must, of course, be thoroughly examined in the final decision, taking into account the proposals and expectations of the stakeholders. The draft under discussion does not provide any certainty or clarity as to who specifically finances what is financed, how much funding, what burden is left on local authorities after reforms, and how much must the whole system state ultimately contribute. The new draft law on basic school and upper secondary school has a direct impact on the quality and accessibility of education and on the salaries of educators, as well as on the subsistence of municipalities and their ability to fulfil their obligations under the law. As a member of the Social Democratic Party, I cannot agree with the situation where, with the entry into force of the bill as a law, the state will start to strangle the schools of small towns and settlements in the counties as well as some upper secondary schools in the county centre. From the point of view of regional policy and equal opportunities, we need to make sure that the new law does not create educational stratification and, due to restructuring, many young people do not simply lose access to education. On the contrary, some form of stratification is created, and there is no mention of reasonable educational arrangements. So, colleagues from the IRL, wherever you are now, replace your minister!
