ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U754	Thank you. I ask for the Riigikogu speech chair in Jevgeni Ossinovski!
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U755	Ladies and gentlemen, In my speech, I would like to focus on a subject which I do not believe has been sufficiently discussed in the discussions on this draft so far. We have discussed the question that, in the spirit of this draft, basic school and upper secondary school should be divided into separate institutions, which, of course, also have a separate budget. The reason for this change is clear: children have been significantly reduced over the last two decades and therefore certain changes to the school network will certainly have to be made. The question is whether the financial strangulation that is currently foreseen is the right way to do it. We have discussed in depth the impact of separation between upper secondary and primary schools on so-called elementary schools. In my opinion, we can clearly see that outside the metropolitan area, upper secondary school education becomes a luxury that most municipalities cannot afford. However, I would like to draw attention to these medium-sized cities in Estonia, where there are now two full cycle upper secondary schools, one in Estonian and the other in Russian. For example, I can mention Stone Oil, Blackwater, Paldiski and Maardu. As a result of the shortage of students, it is already difficult for these municipalities to maintain two upper secondary schools. The planned changes in funding, the content of which has now been postponed into autumn, will in principle make it impossible for local governments to maintain both upper secondary schools in Estonian and Russian at the same time. In educational economics, the most logical decision in this situation would have been to close the Russian-language upper secondary school and direct the children of the Russian-language comprehensive school to the Estonian-language upper secondary school. Russian-language upper secondary schools must teach at least 60% of courses in Estonian as of 2011. In view of the limited resources, it is understandable that the municipality makes such a decision. However, serious consideration should be given to whether such an educational economic decision is also the best educational decision. Russian-language children are directed to study in the same classes as Estonian-language children. If the level of teaching of the Estonian language of Russian-language comprehensive schools were good, it would not be worth making a bigger number of it, but unfortunately this is not the case at the moment. The median result of the 2011 Estonian language exam of the Russian Gymnasium in Kiviõli at the end of the ninth grade was 48 points, and 50 points at the Paldiski Russian Gymnasium. This means that the students' knowledge of the Estonian language was clearly below level B1, which the Ministry of Education and Research has set as a benchmark in order to be able to study in Estonian at upper secondary school. Social Democrats have repeatedly said that level B1 is not enough to study in Estonian at upper secondary school. The question therefore arises as to how children who have graduated from Russian comprehensive school can continue education in their city if, as a result of the changes to be made, they are unable to study even partially in their mother tongue in upper secondary school. Can the Estonian-language schools of these municipalities and the teachers of these schools cope with a large number of students who actually do not speak Estonian at a sufficient level in order to study in Estonian in upper secondary school? Can teachers actually integrate these children into the Estonian-language school environment? I'll take two more minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U756	Two minutes extra time.
ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U757	Thank you. Let's start preparing the vote. I shall put to the vote Amendment 5 tabled by the Group of the Social Democratic Party. Please take a stand and vote. ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U759	It is clear that the successful implementation of such changes will require the help of both the state and local authorities, which will allow the differences in the language skills of students to be levelled out. It is necessary to ensure that support services are provided for those who do not manage well enough in another language environment, to have real opportunities for integrating pupils speaking different native languages and to have bilingual teaching tools available. In addition, there is a separate question with a city like Maardu, where several times fewer students study in Estonian gymnasium than in Russian. In educational economics, it would be right to close the Estonian-language gymnasium in this city and direct young people with Estonian-language language to the Russian gymnasium. The situation is similar in Narva. What am I saying? These are complex educational policy issues, which certainly cannot be resolved from an educational economic point of view. On the contrary, this educational economy, if you could call it that, can produce results that the legislator certainly does not want. I hope that the Committee on Culture will pay particular attention to this aspect when the model for financing school education is discussed this autumn. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2013-06-19_U761	Ladies and gentlemen, First of all, of course, we must acknowledge and welcome the many members of the coalition who sit in the hall or in their rooms and have certainly received a lot of new information during the procedure for this bill and maybe start to wonder which button to press. But when we talk about processing this bill, we cannot get over and around democracy and inclusion. Minister Aaviksoo's high-flying pressure and squirrel tails dragon will be forced upon those in this room today after the adoption of the PGS. This is done in the passions of the opposition and as if to justify something of their own. The minister only announced yesterday in this Chamber that how we understand it is our business, but he understands the bill differently in any case. Classic. Historic memory also remembers that there was once a prime minister on this board who, in response to the repeated complaints of the MP that he did not receive an answer to his question, announced solemnly that what the ambassador was asking was a vigilante's matter, but what the Prime Minister answers is the Prime Minister's matter. It may, of course, be right when looking down from a high point of view, but it is sad that the same rhetoric is used in this very hall 15 years later, and it seems to be normal for the government to communicate with the ambassadors. By paraphrasing a fine statement from our childhood, the Minister of Education often does not know what the Minister of Defence has not learned. The high-flying pillar project was run against the wall years ago, but now a much more broad-based area involving a large number of people has been taken up, and they want to cross this hall with the same crusade. Where is common sense and a democratic process of inclusion in this long-dated bill? Yes, all kinds of interest groups are involved, they've been heard, but it turns out they haven't been heard. In addition, today there is a text at second reading, which most of the people in this Chamber have never seen before, and the Committee on Culture could only see it yesterday. This text, by the way, has not only an educational policy effect but also a clear regional policy effect. These effects will occur in years' time and then it will probably be quite difficult to turn it all back. I cannot be silent if the patronizing message of the minister is that all municipalities in Estonia are rehops and teachers' wages are low in Estonia only because the municipality does not use the money correctly. The state, in the Minister's opinion, contributes enough, just teachers and schools we have too much, and municipalities spend education money somewhere else instead. This bill also makes such a ridiculous move that the term 'pedagog' is replaced by 'teacher' in the text of the law. National responsibility for support specialists, logopaedics, psychologists and specialised pedagogues is excluded. It is precisely for these 'pestors' or non-teachers that the municipality has intended to give this money, but the Minister's message is that it is a distributed teacher's salary. If the concept of assistant teachers or teachers' help disappears from the new text of the law, then rich municipalities may somehow be able to pay these employees differently, but poorer municipalities will certainly find it more difficult and they will simply give up support services. The teacher does not actually get paid, but some of the students lose the service they need. This is, in my opinion, a simple summary of this draft. In addition, of course, it is that class management is accommodated in normal load, etc. There are plenty of concerns, but the finger is pointing at the opposition, because we, like, are holding back the bill by doing our night sittings. In my opinion, the role of the opposition is, in fact, to draw the government's attention to the bottlenecks in its activities. I think the coalition might want to spend one night in front of the mirror and see if this bill is something that adds up. More time, please!

But what saddens most is what makes the teacher's already difficult situation even worse over time. 35 hours of work are not given more precise content. The grades will also disappear. Thus, for example, a senior teacher or a Methodist can be punished for his hard work. The manager must agree on the volume of his very grateful work in the future within the same overall volume of work. The staff of the support structures, i.e. logos, school psychologists, special needs teachers, as well as social pedagogues who have only been recruited as a result of intensive negotiations since 2010 may have to disappear from school step by step. Unfortunately, there are too many questions that are not resolved by this bill. We cannot accept the draft law on basic school and upper secondary school today in such a way that this procedure can go peacefully to the end.
